In the UI: the mode control sits in the composer row beside the harness and model chip, so the recipient and the mode are named before Send (`SEL-8`); compact chat keeps it in view with a pending decision card (`SURF-6`); each sent message records its mode, and expanding it shows the instructions devhost added; the exact labels and recorded wording are 2.12's (`WORD-10`, `WORD-11`). This changes the documented default workflow prompt (`CC-10`), withdrawing the New worktree mode and its `worktree` mode key from `CC-10`, and reshapes `O3`, `O19`, `O20`, `O22`, `O32` and `O35` (every worktree-mode clause there is moot).
  - Consequence: a person who points at the page and sends sees the agent edit the active worktree in place (main's included), with no additional worktree, commit or merge; choosing **Make tickets** asks the agent to file tickets instead of editing.
  - Backend work: the chat send carries `mode` (`live` or `tickets`); the built-in workflow prompt in the manifest's chat settings has one text per mode; `[devtools.chat].prompt` adds the team's guidance first and `[devtools.chat.modes.<mode>].prompt` (`live` or `tickets`) replaces one mode's (`O35`); the session's journal records the mode per message (`CC-40`) so a reloaded page shows it. The stopgap for `journey-agent-fix-01` ships first and makes the built-in prompt edit the active worktree in place, which is **This worktree (live)**, so no release creates worktrees, commits or merges silently while the mode control is built. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q8): that stopgap may change the documented default prompt (`guides/annotations.md`, "The workflow prompt") before the mode control ships, and the docs change in the same release.
  - Superseded worktree-mode clarification (owner item q10): on 2026-11-16 `O1` initially included a third mode, **New worktree (review before merge)**, and on 2026-11-18 q10a2 kept today's workflow (a new worktree, checks, commit, merge into `main`, remove worktree and branch), which Phase 7 asked back against `FB-12` with default q10a4 (starting from and merging into the branch of the page's active worktree, or naming an overlapping uncommitted change) and alternative q10a5 (applying the committed change as uncommitted edits). The owner's comment q10a6 superseded both q10a2 and q4's worktree mode, removing **New worktree (review before merge)** completely and withdrawing its `CC-10` clauses.
  - Network reach while the mode is built (agent-chat research note, question 7). Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item a2): no loopback-only stopgap ships ahead of the redesigns; the agent-chat redesign builds `O36`'s check, which makes remote execution safe while use from another environment keeps working (`HOLD-8`), and the mode control changes nothing about who can reach chat.
- **O2 What Disable Reloads does before an agent receives its request. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-16; clarification decided 2026-11-18 by O56 / q66a2): keep the label Disable Reloads; pressing it opens a confirm popover anchored to the hot reload notice.** The popover names the agent and shows the exact, editable request written for the detected framework (Bun `development.hmr`, Vite `server.hmr`, Next.js and webpack switches), scoped to this worktree, with **Send** and **Cancel**, and offers **Hold Reloads** as the path without an agent. In the UI: the popover anchors to the notice where the notice stack sits in every state: beside the bar with chat docked (`SURF-1`), in the notice's one-line form while the toolbar is collapsed (`SURF-8`), and inside the page's modal dialog while one is open (`SURF-11`); it is an anchored popover, never a modal or an outcome notice (`SURF-2`); **Cancel** sends nothing and starts no conversation or process; the request goes to the agent the popover names (`SEL-8`) in **This worktree (live)** mode (`O1`). The question's focus, default and keys are 2.5's and 2.11's.
  - Consequence: no request reaches an agent until the person has read and sent it; a person without an agent, or who does not want one editing project configuration, holds reloads instead.
  - Backend work: none beyond today's chat path, which already sends the request without the draft or page context; the edited text replaces the prepared one. Who can send it from another environment is `O36`'s check (owner item q46a6, decided 2026-11-25).
  - Clarification resolved (`O56`, owner item q66a2, 2026-11-18): once the Disable Reloads request takes effect, Disable Reloads wins — devhost never auto-reloads the page after a later chat turn, and each turn that changed files ends with "Reload to see the change" and **Reload Page** (`FB-12`, `WORD-2`), superseding revision 4's default of auto-reloading after each turn.
- **O3 The edit mode of a chat message without page context. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q13a1; narrowed by O1 q10a6): the conversation's last chosen mode (This worktree (live) or Make tickets), and This worktree (live) for the first message of a conversation.** For: `O1` names **This worktree (live)** as the default with page context and suggests the conversation's last choice for the rest; the stopgap for `journey-agent-fix-01` makes the built-in prompt edit the active worktree for every message, so this is what people see first; carrying the mode within a conversation keeps follow-ups in the same mode (**This worktree (live)** or **Make tickets**). Against: the second option put to the product owner (the worktree mode for every message without page context) is moot now that `O1` (q10a6) removed the New worktree mode.
  - Consequence: the mode control shows the mode a follow-up inherits before Send; a new conversation without page context starts in **This worktree (live)**.
- **O4 Restart Changed's target when nothing changed. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q14a1): keep the documented fallback, retargeted to the page's service (else the stack's primary service) and named in the label.** For: `stacks/recovery.md` documents a fallback, and Alt+Ctrl+R is what people press to restart the app they look at; the page's service is the scope the crash view and **Reload blocked** already use (`SEL-3`). Against: `services-panel-2-02` also offers disabling the control with "No changes", which would make the key do nothing when nothing changed.
  - Consequence: on the docs page of a stack whose primary service is web, with nothing changed, the button reads "Restart docs" and Alt+Ctrl+R restarts docs, not web.
  - Backend work: the page's service for path routes and `serve` services (`SEL-3`).
- **O5 Whether devhost keeps replacing the app's own 404 and 5xx documents. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q15a1): no; when the app answered, the page shows the app's own document with devhost's notice (`SURF-16`), and devhost's recovery page answers only for what devhost produced or a service that is not running.** For: `error-pages-03` (P1) and `error-pages-04` show the replacement hiding the app's own error and leading with a restart that cannot fix it; `serve-folder-06` shows it replacing devhost's own file server; Vite, Next.js, Laravel Herd and Valet show the app's answer. Against: replacement is a documented feature (`stacks/recovery.md`) and puts the worktree picker in front of a route that lives in another worktree, which the notice keeps only as a collapsed question; an app without an error page of its own shows a bare framework error.
  - Consequence: a build error shows the framework's own error page with a devhost notice; an app's 404 shows the app's 404.
  - Backend work: the document injection keeps the app's body and status and tells the toolbar who answered (`SURF-16`); contract change `CC-4`.
- **O6 What `devhost start <names>` does when the stack runs without those services. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q16a1): start them into the running stack and print what started.** For: `SEL-7` makes the run's selection changeable, as the toolbar's **Start** already does, and `docker compose up <service>` adds services to a running project. Against: exiting non-zero with the remedy (`stack-run-stop-01`'s other option) keeps `start` from changing a stack another terminal or agent started. Today it prints "Already running" and exits 0 without naming the services it left out.
  - Consequence: `devhost start web admin` against a stack started with `web` starts `admin` and its dependencies and prints them.
- **O7 Whether an external service in a repository keeps blocking that repository's worktree switch. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-25, owner item q17a6, reversing revision 5's default): external services (`managed = false`) never block worktree switches, and switching managed services needs no warning about external services staying put (owner comment verbatim: “No need to warn about external services”).** Managed services restart from the target worktree's folder while external services stay untouched (still marked external in the Services panel). On 2026-11-18 the owner submitted q17 blank with a comment that established vocabulary rule `WORD-11` (every working copy is a worktree, main's included); when asked again in worktree terms on 2026-11-25, the owner reversed revision 5's default (`q17a1`, keeping the block) and chose switching managed services without warning about external services (`q17a6`). For (superseded block): an external service, such as a container, keeps serving whatever folder it runs outside devhost, so blocking prevented mixing worktrees. Against (why the owner chose `q17a6`): blocking stopped switching for every managed service in the repository whenever any external dependency lived beside them (`manifest-hot-reload-2-03`), and external services already show as external in the Services panel without needing an extra switch warning.
  - Consequence: a repository that declares external services switches its managed services to the chosen worktree with no external-service block and no extra warning, while its external services stay untouched and marked external in the Services panel.
  - Backend work: remove the `blocked_reason` assignment for `!service.managed` in `apps/devhost/src/services/worktrees.rs:137-141` and update `stacks/worktrees.md` so declaring `managed = false` no longer disables switching for that repository.
- **O8 The narrowest window devhost's surfaces are designed for. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q18a3): the acceptance viewports end at 900x900 and at 1280x800 under 200% zoom (640x400 CSS pixels), where every action stays reachable and no text is clipped, and full pages reflow at 320 CSS pixels (`SURF-9`); chat, its approval prompts and the toolbar's notices also get a phone layout down to 320 CSS pixels, so a paired phone can ask for a change, answer the agent and see the change; below 640 CSS pixels the other surfaces never overflow the window horizontally (a panel takes the window's width less 16 px on each side) but get no layout of their own.** For: every finding measured at 1600x1000, 1280x800 or 900x900; 200% zoom, which WCAG 1.4.4 lets anyone use, turns a 1280x800 window into 640x400 CSS pixels; `HOLD-8` makes use from another environment primary, with the person's other browsers paired (`O36`), so a phone in that environment is a primary case rather than a side effect of Caddy listening on every interface, and the relay docs lead with one (`stacks/remote-previews.md`: "You open the link on your phone, sign in, and try the actual app"); `FB-12` (ask, wait, see the change) needs exactly chat, its approvals and the notice that a turn ended on a phone, over the app page that shows the change. Against: one more layout for three surfaces and acceptance checks at phone width for them; logs, Worktrees, services and the Configuration window stay desk-sized at phone width; a 640x400 viewport still leaves little of the app beside docked chat and a panel.
  - Earlier default: revision 3 had recommended no layout of its own below 640x400 CSS pixels for any surface, because devhost is a development tool used beside an editor; `HOLD-8` replaced it (owner item q18a3, approved).
  - Consequence: on a paired phone, chat, an approval prompt and the notice that a turn ended fit the screen with no horizontal scrolling and no clipped action; at 700 px wide the toolbar shows its summary (`SURF-10`) and an open panel fills the window's width.
  - Applied by: `SURF-9`; agent-chat (compact chat, `SURF-6`) and notices-and-feedback draw the phone layouts in Phase 8.
- **O9 The default restart scope of a manifest edit. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q19a1): keep today's scope (the edited services, their dependents and every service of the same repository) and name each restarted service and why in the reload's outcome.** For: services of one repository often read its shared files, which the repository-wide default protects; naming each restart and its reason (`SEL-11`, 2.7) makes the scope visible without changing it. Against: restarting only the edited services and their dependents restarts less (`manifest-hot-reload-01` leaves the default to the owner).
  - Consequence: a reload's outcome lists every service it restarted with its reason, such as "same repository as api".
- **O10 Lifecycle actions the browser offers beyond today's. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q20a1): a not-started service's hostname gets a status page with Start, the operation the Services panel's Start already performs; the browser still cannot stop the page's own stack; the stack directory stays read-only with links.** For: **Start** already exists for the same service from the Services panel, so offering it where the service's absence shows adds no new power; stopping the page's own stack from that page ends the connection and the control server answering it, and the terminal and the in use popover (for another stack) already stop stacks. Against: Docker Desktop offers stop and start on every project in its list. From another environment **Start** follows `O36` (decided 2026-11-25): this machine and authenticated remote browsers (passkey or paired) press it; an unauthenticated remote browser sees the status page with **Start** unavailable and how to sign in or pair (`HOLD-7`).
  - Consequence: opening a not-started service's hostname shows that it is not started in this run, with **Start**, instead of the generic 404; an authenticated remote browser in another environment gets the same page and **Start**.
- **O11 Whether Make tickets shows the tickets before the agent files them. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q21a1): no review step of devhost's own; the mode's instructions ask the agent to end its reply with every ticket it filed, by title and link, and the agent's own approval of the command that files them is the person's check.** For: the owner described the mode as the agent filing tickets instead of editing, relying on its own skills, and devhost never answers or pre-approves the agent's requests, so the agent's approval prompt already stands before the filing command; a ticket can be closed (`UNDO-5`). Against: tickets are outward-facing and, in a public tracker, visible to others at once; having the agent draft them and file only after the person replies costs one more turn but lets the person edit them first.
  - Consequence: a message sent in **Make tickets** ends with the agent's list of the tickets it filed; nothing in devhost asks before they are filed.
- **O12 Standing approvals granted from the browser. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, reversed recommendation, owner item q22a2): offer the agent CLI's own standing approval on the decision card (such as Claude Code's "don't ask again" or Codex's approval for the session, when the CLI offers one); the person, never devhost, grants it through the CLI's own option, and later matching requests are then answered by the CLI without prompting or a card.** Replaces revision 4's recommendation of no standing approvals from the browser (which offered Deny, Allow Once and Stop Turn only, reasoning that the owner rule that devhost never answers or pre-approves might exclude standing approvals granted through devhost because later matching requests would proceed without prompting or a decision line). For the chosen option: Claude Code's "don't ask again" and Codex's approval for the session spare repeated answers in a long turn, and the person, not devhost, grants them (`REV-9`, `CC-16`, `KEY-9`, `KEY-10`, `review-10`).
  - Consequence: a decision card exposes the CLI's own standing-approval option beside **Deny**, **Allow Once** and **Stop Turn**, with no key approving it (`KEY-9`), and choosing it lets the CLI answer later matching requests in the session without prompting again.
  - Backend work: the approval projection carries any standing-approval option the agent CLI offers for that request, and `respond` forwards the person's choice to the CLI (`CC-16`).
- **O13 Whether turns started from the browser ask before acting by default. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q23a1): devhost passes no approval policy, as today, so the agent CLI's configuration decides, and the composer names the resolved behavior before the first Send, in a warning style when the agent never asks (`REV-6`).** For: the person configured their CLI, and devhost stays out of deciding approvals for them; naming the behavior makes a never-asking setup visible before it acts (`chat-checkout-switch-04`). Against: a page is a new place to start the same CLI, and a stricter default for browser-started turns (asking before edits and commands) would keep them reviewable even when the terminal configuration trusts the agent fully, the alternative `chat-checkout-switch-04` leaves to the owner.
  - Consequence: a person whose Codex runs with full access sees, before the first Send, that the agent never asks and has full disk access.
  - Backend work: reading each harness's resolved approval behavior for the composer.
- **O14 How long a success notice and an Undo stay. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q24a3, calibrated; refined by O53): a success outcome notice stays for its reading time, 1.5 s plus 0.3 s per word, at least 4 s and at most 15 s, counting words as runs of letters and digits; the Undo that replaces a dismissed notice stays 4 s; both count only while neither the pointer nor focus is in the notice and the page is visible, and a notice that arrives while the browser window is not focused waits until the window next gains focus (`O53`).** For: the outcomes the flows write run from 3 words to 39, because `FB-3` names each restarted service with its reason, so one fixed time is either too short for the long ones or idles over the short ones; `notices-and-feedback-03` proposed about 4 s and `reload-and-agents-01` "a few seconds", which stay the time of a short notice; every outcome stays readable in the stack's log (`FB-7`) and a dismissed hot reload notice comes back from the Hold Reloads popover (`UNDO-3`), so nothing is lost when a notice closes; both peer libraries pause on a hidden page or window (sonner 2.0.8 keeps a toast 4 s and pauses while the document is hidden; `@radix-ui/react-toast` 1.3.1 keeps one 5 s and pauses on focus, on pointer movement and on window blur). Against: a notice's time varies with its wording, which is harder to state and to test than one number; the model's constants come from the literature, not from people reading devhost's notices; WCAG 2.2.1 asks that time limits be adjustable, which `FB-6` answers by giving every action in such a notice an untimed equivalent, so this is a reading-comfort default, not a time limit.
  - Modelled from the flows' own sentences, not timed with people, because no notice stack exists yet to observe: the 30 success and neutral sentences that nine proposals say close by themselves, from "Reload notice hidden" (3 words) to a 39-word apply of devhost.toml after a change on disk, need 2.11–11.18 s, taken as 1.35 s to notice the notice (the mental operator of Card, Moran and Newell, "The keystroke-level model for user performance time with interactive systems", 1980) plus reading at 238 words per minute (Brysbaert, "How many words do we read per minute? A review and meta-analysis of reading rate", 2019). Three of them take focus on arrival (`KEY-6`), so their countdown starts only when focus leaves. The rule passes each of the other 27 with 0.58 s (9 words) to 2.02 s (39 words) to spare and keeps none more than 2.02 s past its reading time; on the 4 s floor the 3-word notice waits 1.89 s. 0.3 s per word is 200 words per minute, 16% slower than the mean; the rule meets the floor at 8.3 words and reaches the cap at 45, 1.8 s above the 13.2 s the longest sentence gets. The Undo's paths after its countdown starts need at most 2.85 s by pointer and 2.75–3.87 s by keyboard (from focus outside the notice stack, Alt+Ctrl+B, then Shift+Tab past zero to two other notice actions), from the same model's operators, so 4 s, the rule's own value for its 3 words, passes them with 0.13–1.55 s to spare.
  - Nearest alternatives: a fixed 4 s fails 12 of the 27 timed sentences, every one over 10 words, by 0.38–7.18 s, among them every apply that names its restarts, Restart Stack and a return to the configured worktree (editor hand-off since removed by `O51`); with 0.5 s to notice a notice already in view, it still fails the six from 16 words up, by 0.53–6.33 s. A fixed 5 s still fails 6, 8 s fails 2 and 10 s fails 1, by 1.18 s; a fixed 13 s passes every sentence but keeps "Restarted api · 2.6 s" 10.4 s after it was read, because the needs span a factor of 5.3. An Undo of 3 s fails the keyboard path with one notice action in between by 0.31 s, and one of 2.5 s also fails the plain keyboard path and the pointer path with a hand moved to the keyboard.
  - Pitfalls: counting words by spaces understates a sentence that quotes a path up to three times (3 words by spaces, 9 by reading), so the rule counts runs of letters and digits; restart lists grow with the stack, so a list past the cap is read in the log (`FB-7`); the countdown kept running while the page was hidden, so an outcome that ends "after devhost.toml changed on disk", which arrives while the person is in their editor, closed before anyone could see it; the Undo replacement is smaller than the notice it replaces and must stay under the pointer that pressed **Dismiss**, or its countdown starts at once; a keyboard **Dismiss** has no stated focus target, and sending focus to the next notice or back to the page leaves a five-key way back, so focus moves onto **Undo**; outcomes without a log line (copying, sharing a tab, reconnecting; editor launching since removed by `O51`) must not say "also in the log" in their accessible description; at the reading-aloud rate of 183 words per minute the rule falls 0.10–0.94 s short on 15 sentences, whose readers keep a notice by hovering or focusing it, or read the log.
  - Consequence: "Restarted api · 2.6 s" closes 4 s after the pointer and focus leave it, "Applied devhost.toml: restarted api (changed) and worker (depends on api) · 6 of 6 running · 3.1 s" after 6.9 s, and a notice that arrives while the tab is hidden or while the browser window is unfocused waits for the person to return (`O53`); a failure never closes by itself (`FB-6`).
  - Backend work: none in Rust; the page counts the words of each sentence it shows and pauses the countdown while the document is hidden or for an unfocused arrival (`O53`). It ships with the notice stack, because today's devtools have no self-closing success notice to retime.
  - Unfocused arrival: decided in `O53` (owner item q63a3, 2026-11-18).
- **O15 How long a stopped stack is remembered. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q25a1, calibrated; HTTPS route retention decided 2026-11-25 in O63): 24 hours, or until another stack claims one of its hostnames or the shared Caddy stops; the stack directory lists it as recently stopped only when its run lasted at least a minute, while its hostnames keep their configured Caddy routes and HTTPS certificates active (`O63`) and answer the stopped-stack page for the whole window, relay forms of those hostnames included (revision 7).** For: `stack-run-stop-11` proposes 24 hours, which covers every measured overnight return with 9 hours to spare; the record is small (the stack's name, hostnames, reason, time, folder, start command and how long its run lasted), kept in the state directory; the minute keeps the list to the stacks people come back to. Against: a stack a person stopped on purpose keeps answering its hostnames with a "stopped" page instead of the generic 404, which can mislead after its project moved; a stack whose start fails within its first minute is left out of the directory's list, though its hostnames still name why it stopped.
  - Measured on one development machine's real devhost use, from Caddy's logs and in aggregate: 54 stops of review stacks that started again under the same Caddy came back within a median of 23 s, 36 of them within a minute and the slowest after 19.9 minutes (13 more never started again because their Caddy was torn down); the 8 stacks people came back to had 45 breaks of 5 minutes to 4 hours, and in each of the 11 absences of 30 minutes or more that the configuration log covers, overnight ones included, their routes stayed loaded; with a stop assumed at the last request before each overnight absence, they came back after 12.1, 13.4 and 15.0 hours (and 9.7 hours on a weekend day), and one came back after 27 days (646 hours); the shared Caddy started 8 times in 20 days, 9.0 to 182.7 hours apart leaving out two immediate double starts. No absence from a Friday evening to a Monday morning occurs in the data. 24 hours covers every measured return up to the overnight ones, 9.0 hours past the longest, and misses only the 27-day return, 622 hours beyond it, whose record would name a folder and a command that may have changed.
  - Nearest alternatives: 12 hours misses all three overnight returns, by 0.1, 1.4 and 3.0 hours, the morning-after cases the stopped page exists for, and 16 hours keeps them with only 1.0 hour of margin; 48 hours covers no measured return that 24 hours misses, since none falls between 15.0 and 646 hours, lengthens the list (below), and would usually end with a Caddy restart anyway, as 3 of the 5 measured times between Caddy starts were shorter.
  - The list's condition: 197 of the 205 stacks with an access log were requested in one run and never again, and 181 of the 199 runs of the last 4 days lasted under a minute (median 3 s), bursts of requests under a new hostname with a per-run token, which fit scripted runs rather than people, so another stack almost never claims their hostnames. Sampled every 10 minutes over 4.3 days, a 24-hour list held a median of 34 stopped stacks and a peak of 95 (1 hour still peaks at 15 and loses every overnight return; 48 hours holds 47 and 143); listing only runs of at least a minute brings it to 2 and 5 (48 hours, 4 and 8). Any threshold from 10 s to a minute separates these runs the same way.
  - Pitfalls: requests to a stopped stack's hostnames are never logged, because the site that answers unrouted hostnames keeps no access log and its certificates are cached, so every interval is an upper bound on the time to the next request, which only widens the margins; giving that site an access log, or counting hits in the stopped-stack record, would let a week of use that includes a weekend measure returns directly. The stacks people use did not stop overnight, so the overnight returns assume a stop at the last request, as after an end-of-day `devhost stop`; an idle timeout stops later and widens the margin. Agents started a stack again within 20 minutes or tore down its Caddy, so any window of 20 minutes or more serves them, and a page left open reconnects at the next start inside any window.
  - Consequence: the morning after an idle shutdown, the app's URL says "Acme Shop stopped at 02:14 after 1h idle" with the command that starts it in its folder (`ACT-10`), and the stack directory's recently stopped list shows the few stacks people come back to, not one-run stacks, whose hostnames still answer the stopped page.
  - Backend work: a stopped-stack record per manifest in the state directory, written by the stopping process, which knows how long its run lasted, or by the next devhost command that finds a killed stack's owner dead, rendered into configured stopped-stack Caddy HTTPS routes (`O63`), the static unmatched-hostname page and the stack directory Caddy serves while no stack runs (2.10's Backend contract); contract change `CC-27`.
- **O16 Whether a background tab shows that something needs the person. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, reversed recommendation, owner item q26a2): devhost's own pages (status pages, the stack directory, Watch Live Activity) AND app pages alike prefix the browser tab's title while a waiting agent or a failure persists (such as "(1) waiting · Acme Shop"), restoring the page's original title when cleared (`ACT-7`).** Replaces revision 4's recommendation of changing titles on devhost's own pages only (which reasoned that the devtools stay isolated from the app's title and icon, and that an app's tests might read its title). For the chosen option: a person whose app page sits in a background tab while an agent waits for an approval or a service fails now sees the condition at once without switching back (`ACT-7`, Section 1 ground-rule exception).
  - Consequence: Watch Live Activity's tab reads "Waiting for you · Watch Live Activity" while an agent waits, and an app tab reads "(1) waiting · Acme Shop" (or prefixes its failure) until the condition clears, when devhost restores "Acme Shop".
- **O17 Whose key bindings: the team's or the person's. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q27a1): the manifest's `[devtools.shortcuts]` stays the team default, and the same table in the devhost config file, which belongs to the person's machine, overrides it binding by binding.** For: a binding depends on the person's keyboard layout, assistive technology and desktop (several Linux desktops reserve Ctrl+Alt chords), which no teammate's manifest can know, and VoiceOver users (every Alt+Ctrl chord on macOS) and Windows Magnifier users (Alt+Ctrl+R resizes its lens) get working chords only through a per-person override; `SEL-11` already gives personal preferences to the person with the manifest as the default. Against: the devhost config file holds only the shared Caddy settings today (`CONTEXT.md`), and a second place for one setting asks people to know which file wins.
  - Consequence: a person whose desktop takes Alt+Ctrl+B sets `focusToolbar` in their devhost config file; the team's manifest stays as it is, and the toolbar's hints show the person's binding (`KEY-13`).
  - Backend work: the devhost config file accepts `[devtools.shortcuts]`, and the control server sends the page the merged bindings with where each one comes from (`CC-39`).
- **O18 Process-replacement commands and self-upgrade (`devhost upgrade`, `devhost restart-all`, `devhost stack restart`). Decided (product owner, 2026-11-25, owner item q79a2, superseding q28a1): add built-in `devhost upgrade` and keep shipped `devhost restart-all` (global) and project-scoped `devhost stack restart` (withdrawing revision 5's `devhost stack replace` rename).** On 2026-11-18 the owner approved `q28a1` (`devhost stack replace`) with the comment “maybe devhost self upgrade ? that's the only time this would be called i think”; after Phase 7 researched Rust `main`'s shipped `devhost restart-all` (`apps/devhost/src/main.rs:84`) alongside externally managed installs (dotfiles, package managers, local rebuilds), the owner chose `q79a2` on 2026-11-25: add `devhost upgrade` to download the latest GitHub Release (excluding `media`), verify `SHA256SUMS`, swap a user-writable binary (clearly reporting read-only paths such as `/usr/local/bin` or externally managed installs such as dotfiles or package managers without breaking them or overwriting protected dev builds) and replace running manifest stacks; keep shipped `devhost restart-all` and project-scoped `devhost stack restart` on one verb for dotfiles, package managers and local rebuilds, stating clearly in help, output and docs that they replace the running devhost supervisor process (`CC-12`, `CC-24`, `CC-31`, `CC-36`).
  - Consequence: self-managed users run `devhost upgrade` to fetch, verify, swap and replace running stacks in one step; dotfiles, package-manager and local-rebuild users run `devhost restart-all` (or `devhost stack restart` for one project), printing "Replaced Acme Shop's devhost process: PID 84244 → 85102", and the old terminal of a foreground stack prints who replaced it (`FAIL-5`).
  - Backend work: add `devhost upgrade` (GitHub Release lookup skipping `media`, `SHA256SUMS` verification, writable-path and managed-install guard, atomic binary swap, followed by `restart-all`'s stack replacement); clarify help and output of `devhost restart-all` and `devhost stack restart` (`apps/devhost/src/main.rs:84,609-795`, `apps/devhost/src/services/restart.rs`) to state that they replace the running devhost supervisor process.
- **O19 What This worktree (live) lets the agent do with Git and the person's uncommitted work. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q29a1): the mode's instructions tell the agent to edit files in place and leave every change uncommitted, never to commit, stash, restore, reset or check out files, and, when a fix touches a change the person has not committed, to build on it and say so in its reply.** From the agent-chat research note, question 2; under `O1` (q10a6), **This worktree (live)** is the only editing mode. For: in this mode the person's and the agent's edits share one working tree, and a recorded run backed up and restored the person's own file (`journey-agent-fix-01`); devhost itself never commits, stashes or reverts for the agent (`UNDO-5`), so the mode's instructions keep the agent from doing it. Against: an agent that may not commit leaves no checkpoint to reset to, and a fix that conflicts with an uncommitted change might be better stopped than built on; no run per harness on a fixture with uncommitted changes has been recorded yet.
  - Consequence: after a live-mode turn, Git shows the agent's edits beside the person's, nothing committed, and the reply names any uncommitted change it built on.
  - Backend work: the live mode's text in the built-in workflow prompt (`CC-10`), which the stopgap for `journey-agent-fix-01` ships first.
- **O20 What a change of edit mode within a conversation tells the agent. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q30a1; narrowed by O1 q10a6): each mode's instructions say that they replace earlier workflow instructions for this message, and the composer says when the chosen mode differs from the previous message's.** From the agent-chat research note, question 3 (owner item q30). Because `O1` (q10a6) removed the New worktree mode, a change of mode inside a conversation is only between **This worktree (live)** and **Make tickets**, and the earlier worktree/merge clauses of q30a1 are moot. For: the agent CLIs keep earlier messages, workflow instructions included, in context, so without replacement wording a follow-up switched between **Make tickets** and **This worktree (live)** could follow the previous message's instructions; `O3` sets which mode a follow-up inherits, not what a change of mode tells the agent. Against: the evidence, a two-message run per harness switching modes each way, is not recorded yet, and the composer line adds text for an uncommon case.
  - Consequence: a follow-up switched between **Make tickets** and **This worktree (live)** sends replacement workflow instructions and shows under the mode control, before Send, that the previous message used the other mode.
- **O21 What Make tickets does when the agent cannot file tickets. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, reversed by comment, owner item q31a3): the mode's instructions tell the agent to change no files and, when it cannot file tickets, to say that it cannot make tickets; no ticket drafts.** The product owner's comment of 2026-11-18 on q31, verbatim: “agent should say it doesnt know how to make tickets”. Replaces revision 4's recommendation of replying with ticket drafts as text (which reasoned that drafts gave the person copyable text when devhost cannot detect whether a ticket skill or tracker is configured). Withdraws the ticket-drafts clause of `CC-10` and updates `UNDO-5`.
  - Consequence: in a project where the agent lacks ticket-filing capability, a **Make tickets** message changes no files and ends with the agent saying it cannot make tickets.
- **O22 A record of what an agent's turn changed, and its way back. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q32a1; narrowed by O1 q10a6): each finished turn lists the files the agent changed, built from the tool-call and file-change events the session's journal already holds, with no Git command and no undo devhost performs itself; in This worktree (live) the list offers the reverse patch of the agent's own edits (from each edit's old and new text) to copy and a follow-up asking the agent to undo the turn, never a whole-file `git restore`, and a Disable Reloads result shows the same list beside the agent's instructions for turning reloads back on.** From the agent-chat research note, question 5, and the reload-control research note, question 7, which ask the same thing; the New worktree merge/revert-merge clause of q32a1 is moot under `O1` (q10a6). For: today devhost records nothing per turn and offers no way back, and the docs say devhost runs no Git commands itself (`guides/annotations.md`); the session's journal already holds each tool call's native event, with an edit's path and its old and new text (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/chat/journal.rs`), while the activity log keeps only each tool's name and path (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/chat/session.rs`), so the list needs no Git call; a whole-file restore would also discard the person's own uncommitted changes in a file the agent touched, which Git never had and on which `O19` has the agent build, while a reverse patch undoes only the agent's edits. Against: whether every harness reports every edited path in those events is unmeasured, and a shell command can write files too, so the list can be incomplete; a read-only `git status` would be complete but runs Git and is slow on a large worktree.
  - Consequence: under a finished live-mode turn, "Changed 2 files: src/header.tsx, src/nav.css" with the reverse patch to copy and a follow-up asking the agent to undo the turn, and a note when the agent ran commands whose writes the list cannot see (exact words per 2.12).
  - Backend work: per-turn file lists and the reverse patch from the agent CLI's edit events (old and new text, or the applied patch), after one recorded turn per harness confirms their coverage.
- **O23 What an ended conversation keeps after its stack stops. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q33a1): per conversation, devhost keeps only the harness, its native session ID, the worktree, the model, the last message's mode, the start and end times and a title the agent CLI itself reports, never message text, for as long as `O15` keeps a stopped stack.** From the agent-chat research note, question 6. For: today conversation records live only in memory and their replay files are freed at stop (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/chat/session.rs`), so the resumable ended conversations of `FB-4` and `UNDO-4` would not survive a stop (`chat-conversations-03`); devhost's activity logs and browser storage deliberately never hold message text (`guides/annotations.md`, `reference/chat-api.md`). Against: while the stack runs, the conversation list names each conversation by its first message (`SEL-8`), the label people recognize; after a stop, a CLI that reports no title leaves only the time and the worktree to tell conversations apart.
  - Consequence: after the stack stops and starts again, the conversation list shows the earlier conversations by their CLI titles, ended, with **Resume**.
  - Backend work: a conversation record per manifest in the state directory, removed with the stopped-stack record of `O15`; contract change `CC-38`.
- **O24 What the hot reload notice says after a Disable Reloads request. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q34a1, confirmed): once the request's turn ends, the notice names the request and its result in place of Disable Reloads, stating what devhost observed (the dev server's reload client is gone, or still loaded with the restart the change needs), never that reloads are on or off when the page cannot show it.** From the reload-control research note, question 1, settled by `FB-12` and confirmed by the product owner on 2026-11-18. For: today the notice comes back unchanged after a correct change and offers a second agent turn on an already edited file (`reload-and-agents-02`); detection reads a loaded client, and whether each client's state shows on the page is unverified (Vite still loads `/@vite/client` with `server.hmr: false`); `FB-5` reports this result. Against: a notice that cannot confirm the change keeps a warning on screen after the problem may be gone, until the dev server restarts, and the docs describe the notice (`guides/devtools.md`).
  - Consequence: after Claude Code turns off Vite's `server.hmr`, the notice says the request is done and that Vite's client is still loaded until web restarts, with **Restart** for web (exact words per 2.12).
  - Backend work: per-client detection recorded on fixtures (Bun, Vite, Next.js, webpack-dev-server) before and after each switch and a dev server restart.
- **O25 The Disable Reloads request for a dev server with no verified switch. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q35a1): the popover says that devhost knows no verified switch for the detected dev server, offers **Hold Reloads** as its first action, and keeps a generic request editable.** From the reload-control research note, question 2; it extends `O2` without changing its decision. For: `O2` names Bun's `development.hmr` and Vite's `server.hmr` but only "Next.js and webpack switches"; whether `next dev` has a supported switch is unverified, and webpack pages come from webpack-dev-server (`hot`, `liveReload`) or from middleware with no `devServer` block, so a request that names a setting that does not exist sends the agent guessing. Against: a generic request still works for an agent that reads the project, and leading with **Hold Reloads** plays that down.
  - Consequence: on a Next.js page the popover says that no verified switch is known for Next.js, with **Hold Reloads** first and the editable request below it.
- **O26 Whether the Disable Reloads request may change the manifest. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q36a1): the request names the detected dev server's own setting, says that `watch` only marks services changed and must not be edited, and allows a change to a service's `command` only when the switch is a command-line flag, naming every service that reload restarts (`REV-10`).** From the reload-control research note, question 3, decided with manifest-hot-reload, which owns what a manifest edit restarts (`O9`). For: a manifest save restarts the changed services, their dependents and the repository's other services, the very reload the person wanted to avoid, and `watch` only marks services changed (`apps/devhost/devhost.example.toml`); yet when the switch is a flag in a service's `command`, that edit applied through hot reload is also the restart that makes the change take effect. Against: forbidding manifest edits outright is simpler for the agent to follow and for the person to check.
  - Consequence: for a dev server whose switch is a command-line flag, the request names the service whose `command` changes and every service its reload restarts.
- **O27 Which conversation receives the Disable Reloads request. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q37a1): a new conversation, with each open conversation of this worktree offered by its title as the alternative in the popover; the request never joins a conversation the popover does not name.** From the reload-control research note, question 5; it refines `O2` and `REV-5`, together with agent-chat, which owns the choice of conversation. For: today the request joins the one open conversation, idle included, without asking, where the built-in prompt treats it as more work for that task, against `O2`'s scoping. Against: a new conversation starts another agent process and loses what the open conversation already knows about the project.
  - Consequence: with one conversation open, the popover says that the request starts a new conversation and offers the open one by its title.
- **O28 How long a service with a `process` health check must stay up before a reload counts it started. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q38a1, calibrated): 1 s after it is spawned, unchanged by the calibration; a service that exits within it, with any exit code, fails the start, restart or reload with its exit code and last output lines (`FAIL-3`), a reload rolls back (`UNDO-1`), and an exit after it is reported as an exit after the outcome.** From the config-editor research note, question 6. For: today a start looks for an exit once, at the instant of spawn (`apps/devhost/src/services/health.rs:46,82`), so a command that fails a moment later passes, "Saved and applied." is false and the rollback never runs (`config-editor-05`); `FB-5` certifies a start only once its end state shows. Against: every start or reload of such a service waits the window, one service after another today, and a failure slower than the window, or slowed past it by heavy load, still passes.
  - Measured with a harness that spawns each command as devhost does (its argv without a shell, in its own process group, with piped output, timed to the exit devhost reads), on the fixture's runtimes (`sh` and Bun 1.4.4) on an Apple M4 Pro, 10 runs per step, at the machine's ordinary parallel load and on its efficiency cores as a stand-in for a machine about 2.5 times slower: typical startup failures exit 4–732 ms after spawn (a missing script, module or package, a syntax error, a flag the program rejects, a missing environment variable, a refused connection, a port in use, and `tsc` or `vite` failing with a warm or cold cache), the slowest a cold-cache `vite build` on the efficiency cores; healthy services settle within 453 ms. 1 s catches all 482 runs of the reproducible failures with 268 ms to spare, ends 547 ms after the slowest healthy settle, and sits 1060 ms below the next class, a crash after asynchronous startup work (the fixture's timer crash, from 2.06 s). Every window from 0.75 s to 2.06 s gives the same verdicts on the reproducible failures, the timer crash and a supervisor that never exits, and each wait delays the next service's start, so the shortest window with a safe margin wins.
  - Nearest alternatives: 0.5 s certifies 12 of the 320 failure runs of the per-step ladder as started (3.8%: a cold-cache `vite` 9 of 20, `tsc` 2 of 40, a missing relative module 1 of 20), each showing "Applied" and skipping the rollback; 0.75 s misses none here but sits only 18 ms above the slowest failure; 1.5 s, the alternative if the product owner prefers to absorb rare load stalls, also catches one unreproduced 1030 ms first run and every overload run of a trivial failure (up to 1362 ms) at 0.5 s more per restart, and still misses 2 of 6 overload `vite` runs and the timer crash; 3 s is the shortest round window that catches the timer crash, and it triples the wait.
  - Pitfalls: load sets the tail, not the runtime: medians ran 7–284 ms and maxima 3–10 times the medians, and in an overload spike (load average up to 78 on 14 cores) a trivial failure took up to 1.36 s and a cold `vite` failure 6.8 s, so no fixed window holds and a failure after the window is reported as an exit right after the outcome with its `FAIL-3` evidence; a supervisor that restarts on change (`bun --watch`; `node --watch`, nodemon and `tsx watch` behave alike) printed the error and never exited, so a `process` check certifies it while the app is dead, and the docs should say to run such a service without it or give it a port check; Bun ignores its own unknown flags and runs the app, so a mistyped Bun flag never fails; a cold cache roughly doubles a large CLI's failure time, as on the first start after an install or upgrade, while a fresh worktree alone is not slow (19–39 ms); an exit with code 0 is a failure too (a one-shot command configured as a service); every failing run had its error among its last output lines at exit, so `FAIL-3` can show the cause in time.
  - Consequence: a save that breaks such a service's command reports its exit, with its last lines, about a second later instead of "Saved and applied."; each start or reload waits 1 s per restarted `process`-health service, one after another today.
  - Backend work: the start waits the window after spawn and fails on any exit within it; it ships with the report of an exit after the outcome that manifest-hot-reload's acceptance criteria require, because load can push a failure past any window. Starts and reloads run one service at a time, so k such services add k windows; overlapping each window with the following starts, dependents excepted, would keep the added time near one window (a design choice for the implementation, not a measurement).
- **O29 What Revert returns to in the Configuration window. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q39a1): **Revert** reads the file again and shows the version on disk; returning to what the stack runs is **Restore Running Version** (`UNDO-6`).** From the config-editor research note, question 7. For: today **Revert** returns to the version the window opened, which after a change on disk is neither on disk nor running, yet shows as clean (`config-editor-07`). Against: a person who meant "drop my edits since I opened the file" gets whatever another program wrote since, and a read on **Revert** waits while a reload runs, because it takes the operation lock.
  - Consequence: after an agent changes devhost.toml on disk while the window is open, **Revert** shows the agent's version.
- **O30 How long devhost stop, devhost stack restart, devhost restart-all and devhost upgrade wait for a stack to stop. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q40a3, reversing the calibrated recommendation; parallel-shutdown design settled 2026-11-25): parallel foreground and daemon shutdown from `t = 0`, `q40a1`'s progress line while waiting (`FB-10`), Ctrl-C or a second stop forcing the rest (`KEY-14`), and never killing a stack's supervisor while it is still stopping services, with a 30 s default wait configurable from `5s` to `10m` (owner comment verbatim: “q40a1 but 3 minutes is exessive, up to 30 seconds i think... maybe make it configurable”).** Supersedes the calibrated sequential recommendation (a minute plus 20 s per running service, 3 minutes on a 6-service stack). From the stack-run-stop research note, question 2. For (superseded sequential case): today both commands allow 15 s (`apps/devhost/src/services/stop.rs:20`, `apps/devhost/src/services/restart.rs:27`), while the shutdown itself gives each service 10 s before `SIGKILL` and 10 s after, one service at a time (`apps/devhost/src/services/stack.rs:66,1489-1516`), so `devhost stop` kills the supervisor mid-stop, orphaning services, and reports success, and `devhost stack restart` gives up and leaves the stack stopped (`interrupted-operations-01`, `stack-run-stop-13`); a detached start already allows a failed stack's cleanup a minute plus 20 s per service (`apps/devhost/src/services/detach.rs:36`). Against: a stuck sequential stop waited up to 3 minutes on a 6-service stack, which the owner judged excessive. Contract change `CC-35`.
  - Settled parallel-shutdown architecture (2026-11-25): foreground services (`apps/devhost/src/services/stack.rs:1489-1516`) and daemon lifecycle stops (`apps/devhost/src/services/stack.rs:1567-1622`) run concurrently from `t = 0` inside one shutdown coordinator:
    - **Single termination signal and shared process snapshot:** one process-table snapshot at `t = 0` (`apps/devhost/src/services/containment.rs`) and a single `SIGTERM` fan-out across all managed foreground process trees (eliminating the duplicate termination signal `interrupted-operations-06` where signal forwarding and shutdown both signal the same service).
    - **Bounded foreground stop (15 s ceiling) with shared late-listener scan:** all foreground services share a single parallel 10 s `SIGTERM` grace window, followed by `SIGKILL` to remaining verified process-identity entries (`ACT-14`) and up to 5 s post-`SIGKILL` verification (15 s total foreground bound), with one shared 2 s late-listener port scan ticking every `100 ms` instead of per-service sequential `lsof` spawns on macOS.
    - **Parallel daemon stop (`O41`, 20.12 s ceiling):** all daemon `status` → `stop` chains launch at `t = 0` concurrently with foreground stops under `O41`'s 20 s shared per-daemon deadline (whole process-group `SIGKILL` on timeout; deadline cut read as "did not finish", never "not running").
    - **Combined budget fits 30 s with margin:** `0.82 s` CLI dispatch + `max(15.0 s foreground, 20.12 s daemon)` + `4.7 s` route/record cleanup tail (29 routes) = **`25.64 s`** combined non-hung worst case — **`4.36 s` margin** inside the 30 s default (and well inside coding agents' 120 s Bash timeouts). Expected stop times drop to 3.0–4.5 s (6-service stack), ~8 s (30 services), ~14.5 s (flushes + 12 s compose daemon, down from 25.4 s), and ~14–17 s (3 `SIGTERM`-ignoring services, down from 41.6 s).
    - **Configurable `stopTimeout` (`5s`..`10m`):** parsed via duration string (`parse_go_duration`), precedence: CLI `--timeout <duration>` > `DEVHOST_STOP_TIMEOUT` > `devhost.toml` top-level `stopTimeout` (hot-reloadable) > `~/.config/devhost/config.toml` `stopTimeout` > `30s`.
    - **Interaction with `O41` (`q51a4`):** raising `stopTimeout` does not extend `O41`'s 20 s default daemon deadline; when a manifest explicitly sets `[services.<name>.lifecycle].stopTimeout` above 20 s, the stack's effective wait widens automatically to `max(configured stopTimeout, max_running_daemon_stopTimeout + 10s)` unless an explicit CLI `--timeout` caps the run.
    - **Unified caller deadlines:** one shared stop routine and deadline rule governs `devhost stop`, `devhost stop --worktrees`, `devhost stop --session`, `devhost stack restart`, `devhost restart-all`, `devhost upgrade`, `devhost start --detach` cleanup, Worktrees **Stop Stack** (`worktree-switch-13`), and `devhost serve` sessions. Callers never send `SIGKILL` to a supervisor that is still stopping services; a second Ctrl-C or second `devhost stop` forces remaining verified process trees and daemon stop groups (`KEY-14`) while still completing route, port-claim and stack-record cleanup.
  - Historical sequential calibration (superseded evidence): measured on the review build by sending the stack's devhost process `SIGTERM` sequentially: 6-service fixture 4.6–8.5 s; 30 services 13.1 s; 10 services with 3 s/8 s flushes + 12 s compose daemon 25.4 s (and `devhost stack restart` gave up at 15.35 s, reproducing `stack-run-stop-13`); 10 services with three ignoring `SIGTERM` 41.6 s (each forced service costing 11.0–11.5 s sequentially), plus a 4.1–6.6 s tail (11.6 s on 30 services) for sequential port checks and route cleanup. Those measurements proved why parallel foreground + daemon shutdown and a shared late-listener scan are mandatory to fit 30 s.
  - Consequence: `devhost stop`, `devhost stack restart`, `devhost restart-all`, `devhost upgrade` and Worktrees **Stop Stack** stop foreground services and daemons in parallel, keep one line updated ("Stopping Acme Shop · 3 of 6 stopped · 12 s", exact words per 2.12), finish within 30 s by default (configurable `5s`..`10m`), and never kill a supervisor that is still stopping services.
  - Backend work: parallel foreground + daemon shutdown coordinator in `apps/devhost/src/services/stack.rs` with single signal fan-out (`interrupted-operations-06`), shared `containment.rs` snapshot and shared 2 s port scan; unified `stopTimeout` parsing (`5s`..`10m`) and precedence across CLI, env, `devhost.toml` and `config.toml` (`CC-35`, `CC-39`); `O41` override widening rule; per-service stop progress events (`FB-10`); second-stop / second-Ctrl-C force (`KEY-14`).
- **O31 Whether devhost restart-all and devhost upgrade replace stacks and sessions that run in a terminal. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q41a1; command names updated 2026-11-25 by O18 / q79a2): they replace every manifest stack, foreground ones included, each of which says in its own terminal that it was replaced and where its replacement logs (`FAIL-5`); they list serve sessions in their set and leave them running, with the command that restarts each in its terminal.** Approved as recommended. From the stack-run-stop research note, question 3. For: a foreground terminal replaced today ends with exit 143 and loses its logs and Ctrl-C (`stack-run-stop-02`), and a replaced serve session, meant to end with Ctrl-C (`KEY-14`), becomes a background file server with chat on that no devhost command lists or stops (`serve-folder-03`, `serve-folder-04`). Against: the documented post-upgrade step replaces everything in the state directory, so after an upgrade a serve session keeps running the previous executable until its person restarts it. Contract change `CC-36`.
  - Consequence: with two manifest stacks and one serve session running, `devhost restart-all` (or `devhost upgrade`'s replacement step) replaces the two stacks and prints that the serve session for docs.localhost keeps running the previous devhost, with the command that restarts it in its terminal.
- **O32 Whether devhost prepares a new worktree before switching to it. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q42a1): devhost runs nothing new on a switch; when a service of the new worktree fails for lack of setup, the switch's outcome names the cause from that service's output with the command to run in the worktree (`MENU-5`, `FAIL-3`), and a manifest setting that prepares a worktree is a separate contract decision.** Approved as recommended. From the worktree-switch research note, question 6. For: the manifest decides which commands devhost runs, and a setting that runs commands on a switch would be a new manifest contract, applied through hot reload like every setting. Against: the first switch to a new worktree fails whenever the stack's dependencies live in ignored folders (`worktree-switch-06`); with `O1` (q10a6) removing agent-created worktrees, this applies to worktrees the person creates.
  - Consequence: a switch to a fresh worktree whose app cannot find its dependencies reports the failing service, its last lines and the install command to run in that worktree, then offers **Restart**.
- **O33 Whether multi-stack terminal commands ask. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q43a1; command names updated 2026-11-25 by O18 / q79a2): `devhost stop --worktrees`, `devhost restart-all` and `devhost upgrade` print their set and act; only when the set would end an agent's turn that works or waits do they ask `[y/N]` in a terminal, and without one or under `AGENT=1` refuse unless `--yes`, telling the agent to ask the person (`REV-7`).** Approved as recommended. From the stack-run-stop research note, question 1, whose own default (print the set, never ask, offer `--dry-run`) it left to the product owner. For: a turn cut off mid-edit can leave a file half written (the same note, question 4); plain `devhost stop` points agents at `devhost stop --worktrees` (`apps/devhost/src/services/stop.rs:58`); and a terminal on stdin cannot tell a person from a script, so the command asks only while a turn would end and only when standard input and output are both a terminal and `CI` is unset, and the upgrade step tells scripts to pass `--yes` (`CC-12`). Against: a script run while an agent works fails until it passes `--yes`, and never asking is simpler to document.
  - Consequence: with a Claude Code turn running in a sibling worktree, `devhost stop --worktrees` asks in a terminal and, run by an agent, refuses with one line asking it to have the person decide; with no turn running it prints the stacks and stops them.
- **O34 How long Alt alone is held before Select Elements starts without a pointer move. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q44a4, calibrated, provisional until human holds are measured): 400 ms; a pointer move while Alt alone is held starts it at once (`KEY-2`).** Approved as recommended. For: German, Swiss, French and Nordic Mac layouts type @, braces, brackets, |, backslash and ~ with Option (62 of the 64 layout and symbol pairs in the layouts' own key data), so a hold shorter than a typist's lead before the character key flashes selection over the page while the person types code into the composer; 400 ms keeps the modelled chords clear with 120 ms of margin and still outlines the element before macOS counts a key as held. Against: the typing side is modelled, not measured; a longer hold delays selection for a person who presses Alt and keeps the pointer still; the accessibility critique proposed 300 ms.
  - Modelled typing, from the keystroke operator of Card, Moran and Newell ("The keystroke-level model for user performance time with interactive systems", 1980), with Option counted as a keystroke of its own as the model counts Shift: Option leads its key by 80 ms for the best typist, 120 ms for a good one, 200 ms for an average skilled typist and 280 ms for an average non-secretary typist at 40 words per minute, so 400 ms starts no selection for any of them, 120 ms above the slowest. Measured deliberate side: macOS's default initial key repeat, unchanged on the review machine, counts a key held 500 ms as held, the same half second as iOS's long press, so a still-pointer hold shows the outline 100 ms before a hold registers. For context, the volunteers in Dhakal, Feit, Kristensson and Oulasvirta, "Observations on typing from 136 million keystrokes" (2018), typed at a mean of about 52 words per minute, 231 ms per keystroke.
  - Nearest alternatives: 300 ms, the previous default, classifies the modelled chords the same way but sits only 20 ms above the 40-words-per-minute chord; a typist at that speed averages exactly 300 ms per keystroke, and the 136-million-keystroke study's mean typist is only 69 ms below it on an average keystroke, so every chord that typist types slower than 300 ms flashes selection, how often depending on a spread nobody measured. 500 ms clears the typing rows with 220 ms of margin but meets the platform's half-second hold with none. 250 ms or less flashes on the 40-words-per-minute typist's average chord. No threshold separates a hesitant chord (half a second to 1.2 s, when the person looks for the key, the symbol is rare or the layout unfamiliar) from a deliberate still hold, which lies at or beyond the same boundary.
  - Pitfalls, each a condition any threshold needs, for `KEY-2` and page-context to weigh: a selection that holding Alt started stays on until Option is released, so a hesitant chord's outline outlives the character it typed unless a key that types (a keydown whose `key` is one character or `Dead`, not the arrows `KEY-12` keeps inert) ends it; a still-pointer click comes about 100 ms after Option, before any threshold, so it reaches the page, where Option+click on a link downloads it in Chrome and Safari, unless a pointer press while Alt alone is held starts selection at once, as a move does (today's code starts selection on the Alt keydown itself, `packages/devhost-ui/src/devtools/features/annotationComposer/hooks/usePageSelection.ts:226`, so that click always picks today); 19 of the 62 chords add Shift and 8 are dead keys whose keydown reports `Dead`, so "any other key" means any keydown other than Alt's own, Shift, dead keys and composition included; a `pointermove` can arrive without movement, so a move counts only when the position changes, ideally past a few pixels of jitter; pointer motion while typing, such as a brushed trackpad, starts selection at any threshold; people who use Slow Keys or Sticky Keys press Option and the next key as separate actions, so selection flashes for them at any threshold in the window, which the first condition limits; TanStack Devtools 0.15.0 needs no timer because its inspector's default hotkey, Shift+Alt+Mod, types no character.
  - Consequence: holding Option with the pointer still outlines the element after 400 ms, 100 ms later than at 300 ms; typing @ with Option+L on a German layout starts no selection unless the chord's lead passes 400 ms.
  - Backend work: none in Rust; the threshold is a page constant beside `KEY-2`'s Alt-hold rule, and the acceptance test that typing @ with Option on a German layout never starts selection needs a driver shown to produce a keydown with `key: "@"`, `code: "KeyL"` and `altKey`, or one dispatched by hand, because scripted browser input sends no Option keydown for a chord. Until it ships, today's code starts Select Elements on the Alt keydown itself, so every Option chord flashes it.
  - Blocked: no instrument here produces human key timing; scripted browser input times only its own pauses (leads of 20–105 ms without waits, the chosen wait plus 53–379 ms with them), and nobody typed. The gap is the absence of a typist, not devhost or the review's tools, so no instrument ticket applies. The working measurement is a local test page, never served by devhost: in about three minutes a person types a line of @, braces, brackets, a bar, a backslash and a tilde three times on their own layout and picks one of three boxes about ten times while holding Option, and the page reports Option's lead before each next key and, for thresholds from 150 to 1,000 ms, how many chords would flash, how many picks the timer would start and how many still clicks would reach the page. When a German, Swiss, French or Nordic typist's session replaces the modelled rows, 400 ms stays if their 95th-percentile Option lead is under 300 ms, keeping at least 100 ms of margin; otherwise the threshold becomes that percentile plus 100 ms, at most 500 ms, past which only the first condition protects them.
- **O35 What a manifest's `[devtools.chat].prompt` does now that a message has two edit modes. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q45a4, narrowed by `O1`'s q10a6): each mode's instructions are built in and always sent, after the team's guidance: `prompt` becomes the team's guidance, sent first in every mode, and the mode's instructions follow it and say that they decide where the agent edits and whether it commits or merges, whatever the guidance above says; `[devtools.chat.modes.<mode>].prompt` (`live` or `tickets`; the `worktree` key is withdrawn with `O1`'s q10a6) replaces one mode's instructions, where an empty string sends none for that mode; a manifest that sets `prompt` today gets one warning naming the new keys.** Approved as recommended, narrowed from three modes to two by `O1` (`q10a6`). For: `O1` has every sent message show which mode applied, which a `prompt` replacing every mode's instructions would make untrue (a team prompt that says to work in a worktree, sent under **This worktree (live)**); sending the team's guidance in every mode keeps a team's checks and conventions in every mode; `FB-12` needs a live message's edit to land in the active worktree the app runs, and a team prompt written as a whole workflow, as the built-in one is (`apps/devhost/src/manifest/types.rs:9`), would otherwise come last and could send it into a new worktree. Against: today `prompt` replaces the built-in instructions and an empty string disables them (`apps/devhost/devhost.example.toml`, `apps/devhost/src/manifest/types.rs:9`), so a team that wants its own text to decide where edits go moves it to a mode key; whether every agent CLI follows the precedence statement is unmeasured (one recorded turn per harness with a worktree-workflow team prompt under live mode closes it).
  - Earlier default: revision 3 had recommended appending the team's guidance after the mode's instructions in every mode; `FB-12` replaced it, because a whole-workflow team prompt would then come last and could send a live turn into a new worktree, and `O1`'s q10a6 removed the third (`worktree`) mode key.
  - Consequence: under **This worktree (live)** with a team prompt that says to work in a separate worktree, the agent edits the running worktree, and "Added by devhost" shows the team's guidance, then the live instructions (`WORD-10`).
  - Backend work: the manifest schema gains `[devtools.chat.modes.<mode>].prompt` (`live` and `tickets` only; `worktree` withdrawn from `CC-10`), and the `send` command gains `mode` (`CC-10`).
- **O36 How devhost tells the person using it from another environment apart from other clients. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-25, owner item q46a6, reversing revision 5's pairing-only default): one shared WebAuthn passkey under a single owned parent HTTPS domain, with one-time `devhost pair` links kept as fallback — phone support is a hard requirement (owner comment verbatim: “I'm guessing the pass get here but the important thing is that it has to work on my phone as well”).** Local machine stays trusted so `.localhost`, CLI agents and scripts keep working unchanged: a request is this machine's when its connection and every forwarded hop are loopback (`is_local_client`), it did not come through devhost's relay connector (the connector marks every request it forwards and Caddy strips the mark from requests accepted directly), its `Host` is one of the stack's hostnames, the stack directory's hostname under the local suffix (revision 7: its relay form always arrives through the connector and is never local) or a loopback name, and no browser marks it cross-site or same-site; any other client is authenticated (a valid session cookie issued via passkey sign-in or `devhost pair`) or unauthenticated.
  - Shared-domain passkey architecture and phone requirement:
    - **Single passkey across all machines and stacks:** the person registers one WebAuthn passkey scoped to a single owned parent HTTPS domain (ideally a dedicated subdomain such as `devhost.example.com` so the Relying Party ID stays isolated from the apex domain). Because the passkey's public key material (`credential_id`, COSE public key, `rp_id`, `user_handle`, label) is non-secret, syncing that config snippet in each machine's `~/.config/devhost/config.toml` (safe in dotfiles; synced passkeys keep `signCount = 0`) lets every devhost machine under that parent domain verify the exact same passkey without a central auth server or shared secret.
    - **Phone support is a hard requirement:** works seamlessly on iOS and Android phones via synced passkeys (iCloud Keychain / Apple Passwords, Google Password Manager, 1Password, Bitwarden) and cross-device hybrid/QR sign-in (caBLE), pairing directly with `O8` (`q18a3`) 320 CSS pixel phone layout for chat, approval prompts and notices.
    - **Session cookies and gating:** once passkey sign-in completes on a machine's reserved hostname (or after spending a `devhost pair` link), devhost issues the 30-day idle host-only `__Host-` session cookie (`O59`, `Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, kept in a mode-`0600` per-machine store in the state directory) and propagates it per stack hostname through a hop via the stack directory's reserved hostname, which the relay forwards; relay previews keep the gateway's sign-in in front. Authenticated remote browsers use every control as this machine does; an unauthenticated remote client gets the app and one toolbar state, "Sign in with passkey or pair this browser to use devhost here", and nothing starts. The check gates, for clients that are not this machine: chat sessions, the chat socket, approval answers, the Disable Reloads request, the agent catalog, the agents list, both worktree entry points, restart and start, the config routes, the health, logs and resources sockets, Watch Live Activity (`O55`) and Check Again (`O47`). Whatever the client, the agent catalog's `GET` answers from a cache per stack and worktree (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/chat/transport.rs:88-105`) and starts no agent CLI, and only **Refresh Agents**, a `POST`, discovers again.
    - **Pairing fallback and lockout recovery:** one-time `devhost pair` links (one use, 10 minutes, also printed at start when reachable remotely) stay available for first-time passkey enrollment bootstrapping, machines or hostnames outside the shared HTTPS parent domain (`.localhost`, `.local`, LAN IPs, plain HTTP, or browsers without a trusted local CA), and lockout recovery if a passkey manager is lost or rotated.
  - Evidence & earlier defaults: today the controls answer any client satisfying the control guard's cross-origin and `Host` rules, which read no client address (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/request_guard.rs:7-55`), and a device on the network forges those headers (`network-exposure-01`); every `GET` skips the guard so the catalog starts agent CLIs on every request (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/chat/transport.rs:88-105`, `network-exposure-02`). Relay previews on `main` authenticate only at the gateway: Caddy writes the connector's loopback address into `X-Forwarded-For`, so `is_local_client` (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/server.rs:344-361`) reads every relay visitor as this machine (`running-agents-c5`), and the connector refuses only config writes (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs:21-71`). Revision 3 recommended a loopback-only check; revision 4/5 replaced it under `HOLD-8` (`a2a2`) with per-machine pairing links (`q46a4`); on 2026-11-25 the owner chose `q46a6` (shared-domain passkey with phone support and pairing fallback).
  - Consequence: from a phone or laptop in another environment, the person taps **Sign in with Passkey** once per machine (using their synced passkey or hybrid/QR) and then chats, approves, edits config, switches worktrees and restarts services as on this machine (`FB-12`, `O8`); on `.localhost`, LAN IPs or during first-time setup, `devhost pair` works as fallback; unauthenticated clients see the app and the unauthenticated toolbar state, and nothing starts; on this machine nothing changes.
  - Backend work: pure-Rust **`webauthn-rs` `0.6.x`** (RustCrypto backend, zero OpenSSL, preserving `cargo-zigbuild` cross-compilation), single-use short-TTL challenge store, `config.toml` passkey public key + `rpId` validation (`CC-39`), 4 control routes (`/__devhost__/auth/passkey/register/{start,finish}` and `/__devhost__/auth/passkey/login/{start,finish}`) on each machine's reserved hostname, enrollment and sign-in status pages in `packages/devhost-ui/src/statusPages/`, CLI management and recovery (`devhost passkey register|export|import|list|revoke` alongside `devhost pair`), unified classifier in `apps/devhost/src/devtools/server.rs:344-361` and `apps/devhost/src/devtools/request_guard.rs:7-55`, relay connector's mark and forwarding of the reserved hostname (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs`), cached harness catalog and `POST /__devhost__/chat/harnesses/refresh`; contract changes to `reference/control-api.md` (Security model and status table), `reference/chat-api.md`, `stacks/remote-previews.md` and `guides/managed-caddy.md`. Ships with the agent-chat redesign together with `O39` (owner item a2a2).
  - Session lifetime and idle expiry: decided in `O59` (until revoked, ending after 30 days without use, no fixed lifetime; owner item q69a1, settled by default).
  - Applied by: agent-chat (owns the check, passkey routes and `devhost pair` / `devhost passkey`), running-agents (its local-client flag becomes the three states; the relay mark stays), config-editor (`O38`, `O54`), worktree-switch, startup-and-recovery and services-panel (`O37`), stack-directory (`O47`), reload-control (the Disable Reloads request), log-minimap (the logs socket); serve-folder's sessions share the control server, so the check covers them unchanged; `HOLD-7` describes the unauthenticated state.
- **O37 Who can switch a stack's worktree, stop another stack and restart services from another environment. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q47a4): this machine and any authenticated remote browser (passkey or paired, `O36`), exactly alike, on both entry points of the worktree routes (`/__devhost__/worktrees` and a service hostname's recovery requests) and on Restart, Start and Restart Stack, a chat turn receipt's **Restart Changed** included; any other client gets `403` with "Sign in with passkey or pair this browser to use devhost here." from all of them, runs nothing and receives no worktree paths, owner PIDs or owner IDs (the health socket is gated by the same check), the recovery entry point also applies its service's `Host` rule, and the toolbar shows the unauthenticated state (`HOLD-7`); no separate setting.** Approved as recommended. From worktree-switch's open conflict on `network-exposure-03`, reworked under `HOLD-8` (owner item q47). For: today any client that reaches a stack can switch its worktree, stop another running stack by its owner ID and read worktree paths and PIDs, because the worktree handler checks no client (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/server.rs:374-463`) and the recovery entry point refuses only cross-origin writes (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/recovery.rs:60-115`); the restart and start routes pass the control guard alone (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/server.rs:475-573`); passkey/pairing sign-in is already the person's opt-in, so a second setting would only repeat it; `FB-12` needs the receipt's remaining step: when a turn's change needs a restart, the receipt offers **Restart Changed** or a restart of that service, which an authenticated remote browser must be able to take. Against: an authenticated remote browser can switch the worktree and stop other stacks, the most disruptive controls, with no second confirmation beyond today's; every switch's and stop's terminal line names its origin (`ACT-1`), so the terminal on the stack's machine shows who did it.
  - Earlier default: revision 3 had recommended only a browser on this machine, with an off-by-default setting in the devhost config file beside `stackDirectoryAllowRemote` to restore remote use, because these routes stop and start processes, the boundary config saves keep; `HOLD-8` replaced it.
  - Consequence: from an authenticated phone or laptop in another environment, the Worktrees panel switches and stops as on this machine, and a turn receipt's **Restart Changed** restarts the service, so the change shows there; an unauthenticated browser sees the unauthenticated state and receives no paths or PIDs.
  - Backend work: worktree-switch's Backend 11 on `O36`'s check (both entry points), and restart and start on the same check; the read that stops nothing (`HOLD-4`, `worktree-switch-14`) is now fixed on Rust `main` (`190a8bb5`).
  - Applied by: worktree-switch (Backend 11), startup-and-recovery (the recovery entry point and status pages), services-panel (Restart, Start and Restart Stack), agent-chat (the receipt's restart).
- **O38 Who may read the configuration from another environment.
