Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q48a3): this machine and authenticated remote browsers (passkey or paired, `O36`): with the config editor on, every config route (files, schema, validate, drafts and the running version) answers them, any other client gets `403` with the sign-in/pairing sentence, and on such a page **Config** shows the unauthenticated state and the window never opens; the files route tells the page whether this client may save (`canSave`), which `O54` decides as yes for authenticated remote browsers.** Approved as recommended. From config-editor's open conflict on `network-exposure-04`, reworked under `HOLD-8` (owner item q48). For: the editor is off by default (owner rule, which stays), but with it on, its routes return every manifest and the devhost config file, `env` values included, to anyone who reaches the stack (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/config_files.rs:131,265-295`); `main`'s relay passes reads and refuses writes (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs:21-71`); passkey/pairing sign-in limits remote reads to the person's own browsers, where they want to read them, and an authenticated person can already have an agent read the same files through chat. Against: `env` values reach every browser the person signs in on; the alternative weighed, reads open to anyone who reaches the stack as today, leaves `env` values readable to any device on the network or anyone the relay admits while the editor is on.
  - Earlier default: revision 3 had recommended reads only from this machine, because no other machine could save and the files hold `env` values; `HOLD-8` replaced it, and `O54` (q64a1) confirmed authenticated remote browsers may save.
  - Consequence: from an authenticated remote browser in another environment, **Config** opens with the files and **Save** saves (`O54`); an unauthenticated browser sees the unauthenticated state; on this machine nothing changes; a chat turn that edits a manifest still ends with hot reload applying it.
  - Backend work: config-editor's Backend 10 becomes `O36`'s check on the whole `/__devhost__/config/` prefix, with `canSave` on the files route; contract changes to `reference/control-api.md`'s Security model and the config editor's section of `guides/devtools.md`, beside `CC-19`.
  - Applied by: config-editor (Backend 10; its proposed exception to `HOLD-7` becomes the unauthenticated state); the stack directory keeps its own rule (`stackDirectoryAllowRemote`, `O47`).
- **O39 What `bindHost = "127.0.0.1"` does on macOS. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-25, owner item q49a5, owner comment verbatim: “Pick whichever as long as it preserves a current functionality from the end user experience”): on macOS, honor `bindHost = "127.0.0.1"` from port 1024 upward, rendering `default_bind 127.0.0.1 [::1]` as on Linux; below port 1024 (including default 443), keep listening on all interfaces so existing port-443 setups continue starting without error, while printing an explicit warning on every start explaining macOS's privileged-port restriction and naming the fix (`httpsPort = 4443` or a privileged launcher); every start's proxy line names every active path ("Proxy: HTTPS on 127.0.0.1:4443 and [::1]:4443, direct connections from this machine only; relay previews and SSH forwards still reach it", or the all-interfaces warning below 1024); the default listener stays on every interface; ships with the agent-chat redesign (`a2a2`).** On 2026-11-18 the owner asked why loopback bind had been disabled on macOS; after verifying `apps/devhost/src/caddy/files.rs:497-533` (`render_caddyfile` / `default_bind` dropping `127.0.0.1` on every port on macOS while honoring `::1`), the owner chose `q49a5` on 2026-11-25 to preserve existing port-443 setups without breaking startup while honoring loopback from port 1024 upward and warning explicitly below 1024. Under `HOLD-8`, loopback bind keeps other LAN devices out while the relay connector reaches Caddy over loopback (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy/backend.rs:24-53`), SSH forwards arrive from loopback, and `O36`'s check protects every remote request whatever `bindHost` says. Supersedes revision 5's default (`q49a4`, which refused port 443 on macOS).
  - Consequence: on macOS with `bindHost = "127.0.0.1"` and `httpsPort = 4443`, Caddy binds loopback only, other LAN devices can no longer open stacks directly, relay previews and SSH forwards still reach them, and `devhost start` prints the loopback proxy line; with `bindHost = "127.0.0.1"` and `httpsPort = 443`, Caddy still starts on all interfaces without breaking existing setups and prints an explicit warning on every start naming `httpsPort = 4443` or a privileged launcher.
  - Backend work: listener resolution in `apps/devhost/src/caddy/files.rs:497-533` (`render_caddyfile`: honor `default_bind 127.0.0.1 [::1]` on macOS when port >= 1024; below 1024 keep all interfaces and emit the explicit warning on every start and config save); contract change to the macOS bullet of `guides/shared-managed-caddy-settings.md`; ships with the agent-chat redesign (`a2a2`).
  - Applied by: proxy-settings (step 3's listener check and step 6's warning below port 1024), first-run-setup (`caddy start` proxy line), with serve-folder and stack-run-stop printing what the listener resolves to.
- **O40 Whether `devhost serve` sessions start with chat off. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q50a2, reversing the recommendation): no; a serve session keeps chat on by default as today, stated on the session's first line, with `--no-chat` to turn it off; a `--port` session still never hands an agent the shell's current folder, and who can reach chat follows `O36`'s check.** Supersedes the recommendation (chat off unless `--chat <folder>` names the folder an agent may edit). From serve-folder's open conflict on `serve-folder-04` (owner item q50). For (superseded recommendation's case): every serve session turns chat on today, because it takes devhost's built-in devtools settings (`apps/devhost/src/services/serve.rs:208`, `apps/devhost/src/services/serve_command.rs:96`), whose chat is on (`apps/devhost/src/manifest/types.rs:86-93`), ignores any `devhost.toml` and offers no way off, and a `--port` session started from the home folder makes that folder the agent's; people start serve sessions casually, to preview notes or put HTTPS in front of a container. Against: a person who uses a serve session to chat about a folder would have had to pass `--chat` every time, so the owner chose keeping chat on by default with `--no-chat` to turn it off.
  - Consequence: `devhost serve --dir docs --domain notes.localhost` prints "Serving ~/acme/docs · Caddy listens on all interfaces, port 443 · chat on (pass --no-chat to turn it off)"; with `--no-chat`, the session prints "chat off" and the toolbar's chat entry points say "Chat is off in this serve session (started with --no-chat)."; a `--port` session never hands an agent the shell's current folder; a session with chat on works from an authenticated remote browser in another environment as a stack does (`O36`).
  - Backend work: serve-folder's Backend 5 changes from `--chat <folder>` to `--no-chat` (replacing the proposed `--chat <folder>` CLI contract in `start/serve.md` and `reference/cli.md`), prints chat state on the first line, and keeps no current-folder fallback for `--port`; with a2 answered a2a2 no stopgap ships, so this ships with the serve-folder redesign.
  - Reach: who can reach a session whose chat is on is `O36`'s check, for serve sessions and stacks alike; revision 3's stopgap calls (which coverage would settle this default) are moot with a2's answer.
  - Applied by: serve-folder (Backend 5); agent-chat draws the chat-off reason (`HOLD-5`).
- **O41 The deadline on a daemon's lifecycle status and stop commands. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q51a4, settled by default, calibrated; stack-stop interaction settled 2026-11-25 in O30): one 20 s deadline shared by a daemon's status read and the stop it leads to, counted from the status read's start; a stop with no status command gets 20 s, and so does the status read on the start path; at the deadline devhost kills the commands' whole process group, reports "mail's stop command did not finish within 20 s; the daemon may still be running", ends the operation failed for that service and releases the queue, manifest saves and Ctrl-C; a per-service override in the manifest (`[services.<name>.lifecycle].stopTimeout`) ships in the same release.** Blank without a comment on 2026-11-18, so the calibrated default stays applied and is treated as settled for reconciliation. From services-panel's open conflict on `manifest-hot-reload-2-05`, settled with stack-run-stop. For: today the `status` and `stop` commands of `[services.<name>.lifecycle]` run without any deadline on a stop or restart (`apps/devhost/src/services/stack.rs:1567-1622`), so a 45 s stop silently holds every restart, every manifest save and Ctrl-C, and a command that never returns wedges the stack until devhost is killed; 20 s keeps each daemon's whole stop bounded, where 20 s for each command would double it. Against: a project whose stop takes longer, such as two images that ignore SIGTERM in a dependency chain or a raised stop grace, is reported failed on every stop and may keep running until its person sets the override; the status read before a stop takes up to 0.79 s of the stop's 20 s.
  - Measured with Docker Compose 5.5.0 on Docker 29.8.2 (OrbStack) on an Apple M4 Pro at load average 10.4–21.6, through a harness that copies devhost's process handling for these commands verbatim (the command's own process group, SIGTERM to the group on cancel, the 100 ms wait delay, piped output), 5 runs per container step, 3 per PostgreSQL version and for a container that ignores SIGTERM, 2 for a dependency chain and 1 for a 30 s grace and each hang model, the forced stops being fixed by Docker's own timer: status reads (`docker compose ps`, and a form that exits non-zero when nothing runs) with 0–4 containers took 0.08–0.79 s (median 0.10 s); stops of 1, 2 and 4 containers 0.50–0.83 s, and `down` of 4 0.66–0.72 s; PostgreSQL stopped idle in 0.24–0.35 s and during a 93–159 MB insert in 0.46–1.43 s, its shutdown checkpoint writing 75–100% of the default 128 MB `shared_buffers` in 0.19–0.95 s; a container that ignores SIGTERM stopped in 10.22–10.24 s (Docker's 10 s grace), two such containers in a dependency chain in 20.34–20.36 s and one with a 30 s grace in 30.33 s; a script that never returns, and the real `docker compose ps` and `docker compose stop` against an engine that answers its ping and no other request (a modelled engine), ended only when cut.
  - With the slowest status read added, the shared 20 s passes every stop that Docker ends without forcing anything (2.22 s at most) with at least 17.78 s to spare and one forced container (11.03 s) with 8.97 s; the first reading it fails is two forced levels in a dependency chain, by up to 1.15 s, a project whose stop already costs 20 s every time, which the override serves. A command that never returns is cut at the deadline plus 0.00–0.12 s, so a daemon's whole stop ends within 20.12 s.
  - Nearest alternatives: 10 s fails one forced container, because compose returns 0.22–0.24 s after Docker's own 10 s kill, so a project with one image that ignores SIGTERM would fail every restart; 30 s passes the chain with 8.85 s to spare but fails a 30 s grace by 1.12 s, moving the failing multiple of Docker's grace from two levels to three, and holds a wedged stack 10 s longer; 20 s for each command, the previous default, gives the same verdicts on every reading that ends but lets a status read of almost 20 s and a stop that never returns hold a daemon for up to 40.24 s, and status reads do hang (against the wedged engine, `docker compose ps` waited until it was cut); 60 s passes the 30 s grace with 28.88 s to spare but holds a wedged stack for a minute.
  - Pitfalls: a deadline at a multiple of 10 s fails the stop that uses that many of Docker's grace periods, since compose returns 0.22–0.36 s after Docker's forced kill, never at it; today's cancel path reads a status read ended by a signal as "not running" (4 of 4 hung status reads, and the cut read against the wedged engine), so a deadline added on it would skip the stop and report the daemon stopped, the opposite of its sentence, and compose's exit 130 after a cut is not the cause to report; today's cancel sends SIGTERM to the group and, 100 ms later, kills only the group leader, so a child of a cut command that ignores SIGTERM survived in 4 of 4 runs (`manifest-hot-reload-2-06`); "may still be running" is literally true for compose, because Docker finishes a stop already in flight after the CLI dies (10.41–10.54 s after it began) and compose orders dependency levels in the client, so levels it had not reached never stop (still running 27 s later), and only a fresh status read can say what runs; `docker compose ps` exits 0 when nothing runs (5 of 5), so as a status command it reads the daemon as running and devhost skips `start`, while `sh -c 'test -n "$(docker compose ps -q --status running)"'` exits 1 when nothing runs and took 0.09–0.20 s, which `guides/managed-daemon-style-services.md` does not say; the status contract has no "unknown": an engine that cannot be reached makes the status command exit 1 within 0.15–2.13 s, read as "not running", which is right when the engine is down and wrong when the CLI points at another context while the containers still run; the realistic hang is the engine, not a container, which Docker always forces after its grace, because compose has no request timeout of its own; a database's flush is bounded by its `shared_buffers`, not by its transaction, so a server tuned to gigabytes of them, or a slower disk, flushes longer (modelled, not measured), still capped by Docker's grace unless the project raises it.
  - Consequence: a compose-backed daemon whose image ignores SIGTERM still stops in about 10 s with no report; with a daemon whose stop command hangs on the engine, its row reads "stopping · stop command 12 s", and 20 s after its status read began the stop fails with that sentence while the rest of the stack goes on; a project whose stop takes two of Docker's grace periods sets the override instead of failing every restart.
  - Backend work: services-panel's Backend 4: the shared deadline on the stop and restart paths and on the start path's status read, which a stack shutdown still cancels; the elapsed time it publishes; a cut read as "did not finish", checked before the exit status, never as "not running"; `SIGKILL` to the command's whole process group after the grace (`manifest-hot-reload-2-06`); a fresh status read reported afterwards, never an assumed outcome; and the per-service override (`[services.<name>.lifecycle].stopTimeout`). It ships together with the override, because a project whose stop takes 20.35 s today would otherwise fail every restart in the next release, and runs concurrently with foreground stops under `O30` (settled 2026-11-25: raising `stopTimeout` does not extend `O41`'s 20 s default daemon deadline; when a manifest overrides a daemon's deadline above 20 s, effective stack wait widens to `max(stopTimeout, max_running_daemon_stopTimeout + 10s)` unless CLI `--timeout` caps the run). Contract text in `apps/devhost/devhost.example.toml`, beside `CC-25`, and in `guides/managed-daemon-style-services.md`: the deadline, the override, and a status command that exits non-zero when nothing runs. Until then the commands run without a deadline, as today.
  - Also open: whether a daemon's status command can answer "unknown" as well as running and not running, so that an engine devhost cannot reach is not read as a stopped daemon.
  - Applied by: services-panel (Backend 4), stack-run-stop (its parallel stop budget coordinates it with `O30`).
- **O42 How long a save that no reload has read waits before it says devhost did not see it. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q52a4, calibrated): 5 s from the write, unchanged by the calibration, counted only while no operation (startup, a reload, a restart or a worktree switch) holds the stack, an operation that takes the stack after the write included; then the save's record ends unseen and its line reads "devhost did not see this save; the stack still runs the accepted manifest. Apply it with devhost reload in ~/acme", never "applying" for ever; a version that a later save replaced before a reload read it ends as replaced, never as unseen.** Approved as recommended. From config-editor's open conflict on `manifest-hot-reload-08`, settled with manifest-hot-reload. For: saving a manifest, an include or the devhost config file through a symbolic link starts no reload on Linux (`inotify`) and, after the Rust cutover (`notify` FSEvents), also on macOS (`manifest-hot-reload-08`), and replacing a symbolic link with a regular file stops reloads on macOS (`manifest-hot-reload-10`), leaving the window reading "Saved. devhost is applying the change." indefinitely; the watcher reports a change 200 ms after its last event (`apps/devhost/src/services/watch.rs:20`) and a save waits for any running reload before it writes, so a reload that reads the write starts well inside 5 s; the timer stops when a reload reads the version, not when the reload ends, so long restarts never trip it. Against: a machine stalled far beyond any load measured here could pass 5 s and report a save that a reload then applies, sending the person to `devhost reload` for nothing; a longer wait leaves the person longer with a save that will never apply.
  - Measured on the reviewed build (0.0.12) on an Apple M4 Pro (macOS 26.6.2), from the moment a save returned to the arrival of the reload count that read it on devhost's own health stream, with a probe on the same file-watching library (fsnotify 1.10.1, which uses kqueue on macOS) splitting the time: the fixture's manifest, an include and the devhost config file, saved in place, by a temporary file renamed over them, through the Configuration window and through symbolic links into an unwatched folder, then with a formatter's second write 60 ms after the save and with a program saving every 100 ms for 3 s, at load averages of 11–54, with bursts of 8 niced processes and with devhost moved to the background scheduling band, 301 saves in all: each of the 285 that a reload read was read 190.9–308.5 ms after it (median 204.5 ms, 99th percentile 241.6 ms); kqueue delivered the first event 0–4 ms after the write, devhost's read, the unchanged reload and the delivery took 1–108 ms, and the 200 ms debounce was the rest. The other 16 are not watcher latency: 3 saves made 1 s into a reload that restarts a slow service were read 12.7–14.4 s later, when that reload ended; one made the moment the page answered during startup was read 8.9 s later, when startup ended; and 12 saves after a symbolic link was replaced by a regular file were never read (`manifest-hot-reload-10`). Linux and shared folders were not measured.
  - 5 s sits 4.69 s above the slowest save a reload read, 16 times it, and keeps 3.6 s even under the worst stall the review recorded on that machine (trivial process work taking up to 1.36 s at load 71–78 on its efficiency cores, from `O28`'s calibration); it reports a dropped save 5 s after the write, 5 s before the 10 s at which people stop attending to a display that makes them wait (Nielsen, "Usability Engineering", 1993). The waits behind startup or another operation reached 14.4 s here, and only health timeouts bound them (60 s for the slow service), so no window covers them and the counting rule does; config-editor's rule that a save waits for a running reload before it writes covers the reload but not startup, since the toolbar answered 8.9 s before startup ended.
  - Nearest alternatives: 2 s also reports none of the saves a reload read as unseen, with 1.69 s to spare, 6.5 times the slowest reading, and reports a dropped save 3 s sooner, but keeps only 0.64 s under the worst stall, where a false "devhost did not see this save" sends the person to `devhost reload` for a save that applies a moment later, while dropped saves are rare (a replaced link on macOS, a link on Linux until config-editor's Backend 11); 10 s catches nothing that 5 s misses, since no measured save fell between them except the one during startup, which the counting rule covers, and doubles the wait for every dropped save, up to the attention limit; 30 s, the shortest window that passes the saves made during an operation without the counting rule, is three times the attention limit, and a reload that waits out a 60 s health timeout still exceeds it.
  - Pitfalls: replacing a symbolic link with a regular file, as `perl -pi` does, or writing a temporary file and moving it over the link (macOS's own `sed -i` refuses a link), gives the file's name no event and leaves the watcher on the old target, so that save and every later one, the Configuration window's included (3 of 3), stay unseen until the stack restarts or something writes the old target (`manifest-hot-reload-10`), and on Rust `main` (`notify` FSEvents) symlinked config files miss edits on macOS as well as Linux (`manifest-hot-reload-08`); an operation delays the read by its whole length, because the watcher exists only after startup and a reload waits for the operation lock (`apps/devhost/src/services/stack/runtime.rs:595`, `reload_disk`); the debounce restarts on every event, so a program saving every 100 ms was read 200 ms after it stopped, 3.1 s after its first save, and the versions it replaced are never read; the Configuration window's request checks the file before it writes, 10–39 ms, so the time counts from the write on the server, not from the request; a save from another machine, or from a virtual machine into a shared folder, is never read at any window, because the file-watching library receives no events on NFS, SMB or FUSE for changes made elsewhere, and macOS keeps NFS file attributes cached for 5–60 s by default, so even a poll would see such a save late.
  - Consequence: a save devhost missed is reported 5 s after it was written, with the command that applies it; a save made while the stack starts, reloads, restarts or switches worktree is never reported as missed, its time counting only once that operation ends; of several quick saves, only the last can end unseen.
  - Backend work: config-editor's Backend 12 (the record's unseen and replaced ends, its time counted from the write on the server and only while no operation holds the stack), after its Backend 11 makes the watcher follow symbolic links on Linux and watch a name again after a rename over it on macOS, with the replaced link in its test (`manifest-hot-reload-10`); `devhost reload` is `CC-23`'s. Until then a missed save reads "applying" with no end, as today.
  - Also open: whether devhost watches files on network and virtual-machine shares by polling; until it does, a save made there from another machine ends unseen after 5 s, and `devhost reload` applies it.
  - Applied by: config-editor (Backend 12); manifest-hot-reload reports the same outcome for a save from another program.
- **O43 Whether a deleted running worktree's processes keep running until the person recovers. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q53a1): yes; when a read of the worktree list finds the running worktree's folder gone or unregistered, devhost stops nothing: the repository is blocked with "The worktree ~/acme/.worktrees/fix-nav was deleted; its services still run from it", processes that still run keep running until the recovery the person chooses restarts the repository or they exit, devhost starts none of the repository's services meanwhile and never falls back to another worktree, and two documented sentences change.** Approved as recommended (wording updated to `worktree` per `WORD-11`). From worktree-switch's open conflict on `HOLD-4` and the owner rule that missing selections stay stopped for explicit recovery. For: `HOLD-4` (revision 3) makes a read of the worktree list stop nothing, because reads come from opening Worktrees, **Refresh Worktrees**, a status page's first check and today any client that reaches the stack (`network-exposure-03`), and a read that stops services is how a manifest edit in progress stops a whole repository today (`worktree-switch-14`); devhost still starts nothing from the missing worktree and never falls back, so the owner rule's "never silently fall back" holds. Against: `stacks/worktrees.md` ("If the running checkout was deleted, devhost stops only that repository's services and shows a recovery error.") and `apps/devhost/AGENTS.md` ("A missing running selection stops that repository's services and watchers, ...") change; the processes keep serving from a folder that is gone, so a page may work until the app reads a file it had not loaded; the owner rule's "remain stopped" then means that devhost starts nothing, not that it stops what runs.
  - Consequence: deleting the worktree a stack runs from leaves its services answering, the repository marked blocked with that sentence, until the person picks **Return to Configured Worktree** or another worktree.
  - Backend work: worktree-switch's Backend 2 (a read stops nothing; the repository published as blocked with its kind); both sentences need a `CC-n` beside `HOLD-4`.
  - Applied by: worktree-switch (step 7, Backend 2), agent-chat (**Send** unavailable for that worktree with the same sentence), startup-and-recovery (the recovery view of a blocked worktree).
- **O44 How a route's `path` matches. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q9a1; q54 closed with it): prefix routing: a `path` without a trailing `/*` matches that path and everything under it, as `DEVHOST_PATH`'s documented "public path prefix" reads, so `path = "/api"` routes `/api` and every path below it, and `"/api/*"` keeps working; the docs and release notes state the change. For the part q9 did not ask: a `path` without a `host`, which routes nothing, prints a warning naming its file, line, column and key on start and on each hot reload, never a refusal.** From stack-run-stop's open conflict on `journey-new-project-01`; the product owner answered q9 with prefix routing on 2026-11-18, and owner item q54 closed with it. For: a new two-service manifest put the API on the site's host with `path = "/api"`, devhost accepted it and the toolbar read 2/2 up, but `/api/hello` reached the static site's 404, because a path without `/*` routes as an exact match (`apps/devhost/src/caddy/files.rs:439`, `normalize_route_path`, and `apps/devhost/src/caddy/routes.rs`); Express's mount paths and nginx's `location /api` treat such a path as a prefix, and `guides/environment-variables.md` calls `DEVHOST_PATH` the service's public path prefix. Against: it changes the manifest contract for every route written without `/*`, so a deliberate exact mount (a webhook or health path on a host whose root is another service) starts catching the paths under it, and an exact match then has no spelling. stack-run-stop's proposal recommended a warning instead, naming the exact match and suggesting `"/api/*"`, because it changes no route; this entry departed from it because a warning on the ready line still leaves the first manifest broken and is easy to miss under a detached start or an agent.
  - Consequence: `path = "/api"` routes `/api/hello` to api; a manifest with `path` but no `host` starts with a warning such as "devhost.toml:9:8  services.api.path  routes nothing without host" and "· 1 warning: devhost.toml:9" on the ready line (exact words per 2.12).
  - Backend work: the route renderer adds the prefix match for a path without `/*`, keeping longest-first order; the warning in manifest validation; contract changes to the `path` comment in `apps/devhost/devhost.example.toml` and the manifest reference (a `CC-n` to add).
  - Related: `devhost init` is decided in `O52` (q62a1).
  - Applied by: stack-run-stop (Backend 15, whose warning for a path without `/*` gives way to prefix matching in Phase 8, and Backend 16 for `devhost init`); `O4`'s page service, resolved from the route that answered, follows the new matching.
- **O45 How a service learns its public URL. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q55a3, reversing the recommendation): neither `publicUrl` nor `DEVHOST_PUBLIC_URL`; keep today's behavior.** Supersedes the recommendation (adding a `publicUrl` service reference to `guides/service-references.md`). From proxy-settings' open conflict on `proxy-settings-06`. For (superseded recommendation's case): with an HTTPS port other than 443, a service cannot learn its own public URL, and a port change leaves hard-coded ones stale; the finding suggested a `DEVHOST_PUBLIC_URL` variable for every routed service, which would restart every routed service on an `httpsPort` change, against the shared Caddy settings' "Services, routes, terminals, and chat sessions keep running."; a `publicUrl` reference would have restarted only services that opted into it. Against: adding a new reference or variable expands the manifest contract and asks teams to wire it; the owner chose to keep today's behavior unchanged.
  - Consequence: changing `httpsPort` restarts no services, and no `publicUrl` reference or `DEVHOST_PUBLIC_URL` variable is added.
  - Backend work: none; proxy-settings' Backend 10 (`publicUrl` reference, restart set, and save statement) and the proposed contract change to `guides/service-references.md` are withdrawn.
  - Applied by: proxy-settings (withdraws step 7's `publicUrl` addition and Backend 10); config-editor's save statement lists no `publicUrl` restart.
- **O46 How close a mistyped hostname must be for the Caddy 404 and stack directory to offer it. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q56a4, calibrated; HTTPS/HTTP scope settled 2026-11-25 in O63): a requested hostname that belongs to a stack devhost remembers as stopped is named as that stopped stack before any matching (and stays reachable over HTTPS via its active configured route for `O15`'s 24 h window under `O63`); otherwise, on plain HTTP (when `httpEnabled = true`) and in the stack directory (`O63`), devhost offers each routed hostname within two edits of the request, measured on the name before the shared suffix by optimal string alignment (a character added, removed or changed, or two neighbouring characters swapped, each one edit), within one edit when that routed name has four characters or fewer, and never the same name with a different number (s07 never offers s01); at most three offers, closest first, ties in alphabetical order, each a link that keeps the path and query; arbitrary unknown HTTPS hostnames keep strict configured-hosts-only TLS (`O63`).** Approved as recommended. From stack-directory's open conflict on `stack-directory-04`. For: today the 404 for an unrouted hostname never names it, suggests nothing and lists every routed hostname from A to Z, so the person compares names letter by letter and types the lost path again; one or two edits cover the common slips (a swap such as hotfix-shpo for hotfix-shop, a doubled or dropped letter), and when nothing is offered the page still lists every hostname, grouped by stack. Against: a wrong offer is a link to a different running service that looks like the one the person asked for, which is why short and numbered names get the narrower rules; a two-error typo of a four-letter name, and a stale bookmark after most renames, get no offer.
  - Measured without a record of the 404's own requests (its catch-all site keeps no access log), from the certificates Caddy issues on demand for the first request to each hostname, set against every hostname ever routed. In the review's 46 isolated environments, 81 requested hostnames were never routed: the reviewers' 3 deliberate mistypes, each 1 edit from the routed name; 36 numbered services of a 40-service test stack, requested while only its first four were routed, 21 of them 1 edit and 15 2 edits from a routed sibling; and 42 names 4 or more edits from anything. On one development machine's real devhost use, in aggregate: 223 hostnames routed ever, 27 of them chosen by people (names of 4 to 27 characters before the suffix, in 21 projects) and 196 generated labels of 45 to 55 characters; of 195 hostnames requested but never routed, 3 were 2 edits (a letter added and one changed) from a name first served under 3 minutes later, and 192 were 4 or more edits from every routed name; the closest distinct chosen names there are also 2 edits apart, so 4 of 223 requests for a stopped project's own names would offer a neighbour. 12 renames of `.localhost` hostnames in this repository's history stand for stale bookmarks: 3 at 1 edit and 9 at 3–10. A typing model added every single error of four kinds on each name (a character deleted, a doubled or neighbouring key inserted, a neighbouring key substituted, two neighbouring keys swapped, on a US QWERTY layout) and 200 sampled two-error typos per name, weighted 0.8 to 0.2 after Damerau, who found over 80% of misspellings to be one such error ("A technique for computer detection and correction of spelling errors", 1964), over the documentation's 13 example names, a modelled machine of 14 names in 5 stacks and that development machine's 27 chosen names.
  - Names of five characters or more get two edits: the real mistypes were 1 edit (a margin of 1) and 2 edits (a margin of 0), and one edit misses all three of that machine's near misses and every two-error typo, about 20% of weighted mistypes; above, distance leaves no margin, since that machine's closest distinct chosen names are also 2 apart, and the stopped-stack record, not a distance, separates a near miss from a stopped project's own name, turning those 4 wrong offers of 223 into none. Names of four characters or fewer get one edit: the documentation's app is 3 edits from web and from shop (a margin of 2), the modelled db is 2 from web (a margin of 1) and api 1 from app; one edit makes no wrong one-error offer on the documentation's examples (0 of 1448, against 11 at two edits) and stops offering db when web is stopped, and fails only where api and app tie at one edit (18 wrong of 1532), a tie no threshold breaks, so the page offers both. Its cost is the two-error typos of four-letter names, which keep today's full list: 1.35% of weighted mistypes on the modelled machine, 0.64% on that development machine's chosen names and 3.9% on the documentation's examples, where 3 of 13 names are short. Numbers: every distance limit offers a routed sibling for the numbered services (21 wrong at one edit, 36 at two), and the rule against the same name with a different number answers all 36, at the cost of 88 of 6876 one-error typos on that machine's chosen names (1.3%), those that turn one number into another, where masking every number would cost 244; with both rules, 1.69% of that machine's weighted mistypes get no offer and 0.02% a wrong one. The cap of three never bound under this rule, so it is a safe ceiling, not a calibrated count.
  - Nearest alternatives: today's two edits for every name answers every real numbered request wrongly (36 of 36), offers a different or unrelated short name for 0.82% of the documentation's weighted mistypes and 8.1% once db and api are routed, and offers db when web is stopped; two edits with the number rule but without the short-name rule answers the numbered requests and misses nothing on the modelled machine, but keeps every short-name wrong offer (11 of 1448 one-error typos of the documentation's examples, 133 of 1532 with db and api); one edit misses every two-error typo and that machine's three near misses, and still offers a sibling for 21 of the 36 numbered services; three edits offers more wrong names wherever short names exist (101 of 1448 one-error typos of the documentation's examples, 5 of 13 stopped examples' names) and gains one stale bookmark of 12 while offering two wrong names; Levenshtein distance, which the pre-Rust CLI used through cobra (now clap in `apps/devhost/src/cli.rs`), counts a swap as two edits, so the entry's own hotfix-shpo is 2 away and a swap plus one more error is missed (196 of 2613 and 445 of 5132 two-error typos), and a prefix rule offers every name that begins with the request, so a stopped `api` would offer `api-hello`.
  - Pitfalls: numbered siblings look like typos, so the matcher compares names with their numbers masked first; a stopped stack's own hostname is not a typo, so matching starts from the stopped-stack record, which `O15` keeps 24 hours, and beyond them those 4 wrong offers of 223 return; stale bookmarks are mostly far (9 of 12 renames at 3–10 edits) while the newest were near (three renames from a dot to a hyphen, such as api.shop to api-shop, 1 edit each), and two old hostnames now route elsewhere, so a bookmark to them opens another page with no 404 for any matcher to see; most hostnames are generated and nobody types them (196 of 223 routed names on that machine are 45–55 characters and 189 of 195 unrouted ones 36, a UUID's length), eight times the names people chose, so the matcher skips a name whose length differs from the request's by more than the limit before it computes a distance; all three near misses came under 3 minutes before the name they resemble was first served, so when that stack is not yet routed no limit can offer it, and the page's sentence on starting a stack is the answer; distance on the whole hostname hides short names, so "four characters or fewer" is measured before the shared suffix; a certificate records only a hostname's first request, so repeated requests, plain-HTTP requests and requests for a name routed at another time are invisible, and intent was inferred from shape and timing, which a rotated access log on the 404 site, read over a week of use, would measure directly.
  - Consequence: opening hotfix-shpo.shop.localhost/checkout on plain HTTP (when `httpEnabled = true`) or searching it in the stack directory offers "Did you mean hotfix-shop.shop.localhost/checkout?" as the first link; db.localhost while only web.localhost is routed offers nothing; a request for s07 of a stack that routes s01 to s04 offers nothing; a stopped stack's hostname names that stack and how to start it over HTTPS (`O63`) instead of offering a neighbour; with no offer the page lists every hostname, as today.
  - Backend work: the route data the unmatched-hostname page and stack directory embed, consulting the stopped-stack record of `O15` (`CC-27`, `O63`) first and masking numbers before matching; the short `404` for requests that are not page loads names the same offers; stack-directory's matcher test gains three cases: a four-letter name is not offered at two edits, s07 does not offer s01, and a stopped stack's hostname names the stack instead of a neighbour. It ships with or after that record, without which 4 of 223 requests for a stopped project's own names on that machine would offer a neighbour. Until then the 404 offers nothing, as today.
  - Applied by: stack-directory (step 8 and its matcher test), bounded on HTTPS by `O63`.
- **O47 Who may read the stack directory and press Check Again from another environment. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q57a3): authenticated remote browsers (passkey or paired, `O36`) read the directory and press **Check Again**, as this machine does; readers admitted only by `stackDirectoryAllowRemote` keep reading it anonymously and see **Check Again** unavailable with "Sign in with passkey or pair this browser to check stacks from here"; the relay forwards the stack directory's hostname, so an authenticated remote browser also reaches it through a relay preview.** Approved as recommended. From stack-directory's open conflict on its Check Again route, reworked under `HOLD-8` (owner item q57). For: the directory is local by default and readable from other devices only with `stackDirectoryAllowRemote`, with no sign-in (`guides/managed-caddy.md`: "That page accepts local clients by default; `[caddy].stackDirectoryAllowRemote = true` in `config.toml` enables reading it from other devices."; `apps/devhost/src/devtools/directory.rs:34-39`), and its hostname is not relayed (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs:21-71`); **Check Again** makes a stack act (it probes the addresses its manifest names and reads Git's worktree list, which stops nothing now that `worktree-switch-14` is fixed on Rust `main` `190a8bb5`); passkey/pairing sign-in admits the person's own browsers without opening the directory to the network, and the change is additive: `stackDirectoryAllowRemote` keeps meaning anyone, read-only, no sign-in. Against: every authenticated browser can make stacks re-run their checks; the relay forwards one more hostname, as `O55` and `O36`'s reserved-host sign-in hop also need.
  - Earlier default: revision 3 had recommended **Check Again** only on this machine, because the directory promises remote readers a read-only page and Check Again makes stacks act; `HOLD-8` replaced it.
  - Consequence: on an authenticated laptop or phone in another environment, the directory lists the stacks and **Check Again** refreshes one; an anonymous reader admitted by `stackDirectoryAllowRemote` sees "Sign in with passkey or pair this browser to check stacks from here"; a chat turn's change still shows on the page it came from, not here.
  - Backend work: `GET /stacks.json?check=<stack>` answered under `O36`'s check (the check covers gated routes whatever their method), added with services-panel's Check Again route to `CC-37`'s table; the directory's reads gain authenticated remote browsers; the relay forwards the stack directory's hostname.
  - Applied by: stack-directory (its Check Again route, the unauthenticated state, its reach through the relay); services-panel's own Check Again, on the stack's hostname, follows `O37`.
- **O48 How the toolbar's CPU, RAM and Disk readouts set their levels. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q58a4, calibrated): CPU averages its readings of the last 20 s and reads high at 70% (back to normal below 65%) and very high at 90% (back to high below 85%), with no separate hold; RAM takes each reading as it comes and reads high at 75% (back to normal below 70%) and very high at 90% held for 10 s (back to high below 85%), the very-high line uncalibrated; Disk shows free space and, on a disk of any size, reads low below 20 GiB free and very low below 10 GiB free, each level clearing only once free space is 1 GiB above its floor; a level that is not normal shows its word and a mark of its own, never a service state's glyph or the failed look.** Approved as recommended. From toolbar-shell's open conflict on `toolbar-shell-04`. For: today every 2 s reading sets a readout's color at 70% and 90% (`packages/devhost-ui/src/devtools/features/resourceUsage/constants.ts:2`), so a reading that swings around a line turns amber and red and back from one reading to the next, and Disk is judged by the share used, so a large disk with tens of GiB free reads red for good; a 20 s average with lower lines to fall back below gives a level that settles, a RAM line set against the kernel's own memory pressure marks little ordinary time high, and fixed floors alarm when free space is short on a disk of any size. Against: a 20 s average shows a real spike as high up to 6 s later than a 10 s one, and a 10 s burst never reaches very high; a fast disk fill reads normal until it crosses a floor; RAM's share does not say how short of memory a machine is, and its 75% line is calibrated on one 48 GiB Mac.
  - Recorded every 2 s with the reviewed build's sampler (gopsutil 4.26.9; Rust `main` now samples the same OS counters via `sysinfo` in `apps/devhost/src/devtools/resources.rs`) on an Apple M4 Pro (10 performance and 4 efficiency cores, 48 GiB, a 460.4 GiB data container, macOS 26.6.2), each sample with the 1-minute load and the kernel's memory pressure level, in three recordings of 11–12 minutes while other work ran beside them: the machine as it is (load average 9.1–22.8, CPU at a median 59%), real `bun build` production builds by 6 niced workers in episodes of 10 to 60 s, and an edit-and-test loop recompiling one package every 10 s (46 runs); 1020 samples in all, each rule replayed over them and over synthetic steps to 95% and 100% from 10%, 40% and 60% for its delays. devhost's own stream, logged beside the recorder for 8.3 minutes, gave 10 s CPU averages within 1.66 points of it on average (correlation 0.989), the same RAM readings except one sudden drop read 1.2 s apart, and the same Disk readings. Disk was also judged on 28 readings of that machine's free space over 2.5 days, from 0 to 75 GiB, each with what happened then: 5 that needed action (a full disk, or below the 10 GiB at which work was paused; 0–4.3 GiB), 16 of ordinary work (18–75 GiB) and 7 while another session filled the disk at about 4 GiB a minute (71 down to 27 GiB); disks of 256 GB, 512 GB, 1 TB and 2 TB are modelled.
  - CPU: 20 s is the shortest average that undid no level in under 10 s in any recording, its closest undo 22 s apart as is, 10 s during the builds (a real change of load between two episodes) and 24 s during the tests; it shows a saturating spike very high 16–18 s after it starts (17–18 s into the real 20, 30 and 60 s builds), no later than the previous default's 10 s average with its 10 s hold (18–20 s), because the long average itself needs most of 20 s near saturation and so replaces the hold, and high 6–14 s after the start against 4–8 s, up to 6 s later; a 10 s burst reaches very high under no averaged rule.
  - RAM: at normal pressure it read 53.8–78.0% (median 63.8%), and under the kernel's warning pressure, which lasted about 8 minutes, 74.2–83.3% (median 78.8%); high at 75% (back below 70%) is the only line that showed the whole warning, turning high 24 s before the kernel's warning began, while marking 7.3% of normal-pressure time high; it changed level 0.17 times a minute as is and undid nothing in under 10 s, so RAM needs no average. Very high keeps 90% held 10 s, uncalibrated: the kernel never reported critical pressure, the highest reading under warning pressure was 83.3%, and producing critical pressure on a shared machine would starve other programs.
  - Disk: very low below 10 GiB marks all 5 readings that needed action (the highest, 4.3 GiB, is 5.7 GiB below it) and none of the 16 ordinary ones (the lowest, 18 GiB, is 8 GiB above it), and lasts 2–3 minutes, two or three of Disk's one-minute readings, at the fastest fill measured (3.5–5 GiB a minute); low below 20 GiB marks 1 of the 16 ordinary readings (18 GiB, a heads-up), leaves that machine's usual 21–31 GiB normal and gives two readings between low and very low at the fastest fill; the 1 GiB band cut the 20 GiB floor's level changes in the one-minute recordings from 3 to 1, and a floor at the recordings' median free space, the worst case, changed level 3 times with no band or 0.5 GiB and once with 1 or 2 GiB.
  - Nearest alternatives: for CPU, the previous 10 s average undid 7 changes in under 10 s as is, 1 during the builds and 2 during the tests; 15 s undid one, after 4 s during the builds with the hold and after 6 s during the tests without it; a 20 s average keeping the 10 s hold is as steady but shows very high only 26–28 s into a spike, so a 30 s build reads very high at its end (27 s); without the lower lines the 15 s and 20 s averages undo 5 changes in under 10 s as is; a 10 s average whose level falls only after 20 s below its line is as steady but shows high for 51% of the time as is while 29% of the readings were at 70% or more, so its word contradicts its figure ("CPU 45% high"). For RAM, high at 70% with its lower line at 65%, the previous default, marks 33.8% of normal-pressure time high, and today's 70% per reading 19.6%; 80% marks no normal time but shows only 62.7% of the warning and turns high 176 s after it began, and 85% never fires. For Disk, the previous shares (low below 10% free, very low below 5% or 10 GiB) alarm on 12 of that machine's 16 ordinary readings, 6 of them very low, read very low through every recording because 5% of 460 GiB is 23 GiB, and on large disks turn very low at 46 GiB on 1 TB and 92 GiB on 2 TB, 12 and 23 minutes from full at 4 GiB a minute, agreeing with the floors only on small disks (23.6 and 11.8 GiB at 256 GB); very low below 5 GiB lasts 60–85 s at the fastest fill, less than two readings, so the readout can go from low to full without showing very low; low below 15 GiB leaves about one reading between the lines, so it can skip low; low below 25 GiB marks 7 of the 16 ordinary readings, that machine's everyday state.
  - Pitfalls: that machine is never idle: as is, CPU crossed 70% 66 times in 12 minutes, once every 11 s, and during the builds and tests other programs held it near 100% at a load average up to 83 on 14 cores, so on a busy machine the readouts mostly measure other programs, and the stack's share in the details (toolbar-shell's Backend 7) is what tells a person whether the stack is the cause; the proposal's acceptance test, 65% and 95% alternating every 2 s, changes the level once under every rule with lower lines, 2 s readings with no average included, which undid 40 changes in under 10 s on the real machine, so the flicker test replays a recorded trace and asserts that no change is undone in under 10 s; the window is time, not a count of readings, since `pollInterval` accepts 250 ms and up, and the stream carries no time stamp and sends each CPU reading twice (after the CPU read and again after the RAM read), so the page stamps each arrival, counts each distinct reading once and leaves out the first CPU reading after devhost starts, which covers the time since its process started; devhost's stack and the recorder, reading the same machine about 1 s apart, gave different levels 15% of the time under today's per-reading rule and 8% with averaging; RAM readings of 74.2–78.0% occurred under both normal and warning pressure, so no share separates the two, while the kernel publishes the exact state (`kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level` on macOS, the memory pressure-stall figures in `/proc/pressure/memory` on Linux); a fast fill is invisible to any floor (71 to 27 GiB in 11 minutes read normal throughout), so the Disk details could show the change over the last minutes; devhost's free space leaves out purgeable space, 2.24 GB less than Finder showed on that disk, which the details say.
  - Consequence: a build that holds CPU near 70% shows "CPU 72% high" once instead of flickering, and a saturating build reads very high about 17 s in; a 994 GiB disk with 27 GiB free reads "Disk 27 GiB free" with no level, as toolbar-shell's mockup shows, and once it falls below 10 GiB free it reads very low until 11 GiB are free again.
  - Backend work: none for the levels, which the page computes from the readings it already receives, stamping their arrival; the stack's share in the readout's details is toolbar-shell's Backend 7, beside `CC-26`. It ships with toolbar-shell's readouts (step 1), whose acceptance criteria change with it: 92% held for 20 s shows very high, the flicker test replays a recorded trace, Disk reads low below 20 GiB and very low below 10 GiB on any disk with the 1 GiB band, and RAM's lines are 75% and 70%. Until then each 2 s reading sets the color at 70% and 90%, as today.
  - Also open: whether RAM's level comes from the kernel's own memory pressure instead of a share, which would end the overlap and hold on machines with less memory, where the evidence so far is one 48 GiB Mac; a working day's recording on a 16 GiB Mac and on Linux, and a large build on a 1 TB or 2 TB disk, would calibrate RAM's lines, its very-high line included, and the modelled disks.
  - Applied by: toolbar-shell (step 1's readouts and their unit tests).
- **O49 How many changes the page keeps for each Zustand store before the Redux DevTools window opens. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q59a4, calibrated): the latest 50 per store, kept from the store's registration, so a window opened later lists them and keeps them across closing and reopening, within a limit of 16 MB of kept snapshots per store, counting two bytes per character (8 million characters); consecutive equal snapshots share one kept string; while the window is closed, a store whose latest snapshot leaves room under the limit for fewer than two changes (over 4 million characters) keeps none, and its history starts when the window connects; whenever the count or the limit has dropped older changes, the window says how many it shows ("Zustand cache: showing its latest 7 changes"), and where a store kept none, that its history starts at connection.** Approved as recommended. From third-party-devtools' open conflict on `third-party-devtools-03`. For: today a Zustand store's history starts when the window connects, so every reopen or page reload starts it over; 50 is the Redux DevTools extension's default `maxAge` and the bound devhost's Zustand producer already sends the window (`packages/devhost-ui/src/devtools/features/reduxDevtools/createZustandStoreProducer.ts:77`), so a larger count would be cut back to 50 unless that changes too; the limit leaves every store up to 160 KB its full 50 and caps the large stores that a count alone lets hold 55–276 MB from registration. Against: every change of every registered store is serialized from its registration, where today a closed window costs nothing; a large store keeps few changes (7 of a 1.1 MB state); a longer history helps when debugging a long session.
  - Measured in HeadlessChrome 157 on an Apple M4 Pro (48 GiB, macOS 26.6.2), on a local page that builds exactly the producer's message for each change of real Zustand 5.0.15 stores, serialized by the window's own serializer (`@redux-devtools/app-core` 3.0.0, with jsan 3.1.14), and reads the heap after forced collection, three runs per step: a kept change costs its serialized size plus about 0.15 KB, so 50 changes held 0.003–0.005 MB for the fixture's counter (13 B, in the noise), 0.047 MB for an 8-field sign-up form (0.8–1.0 KB), 0.46 MB for a 70-field settings form (9.1–9.3 KB), 5.3 MB for a list of 300 records (106 KB), 54.7 MB for a normalized API cache (1.09 MB) and 276.3 MB for one five times larger (5.53 MB); one curly apostrophe in every 50 posts doubled the 1.09 MB cache's 50 to 108.5 MB, because V8 stores a string with any character outside Latin-1 at two bytes per character. Serializing one change took a median 0.4 ms for the list, 2.9 ms for the 1.09 MB cache and 16.7 ms for the 5.53 MB cache (95th percentile 19.5 ms, at most 35.7 ms), at load averages of 13–18. Every keystroke, click, poll and pointer move made exactly one change, and a drag delivered 59.3–59.9 changes a second at 60 frames a second; at the mean typing speed of 51.56 words per minute (Dhakal, Feit, Kristensson and Oulasvirta, "Observations on typing from 136 million keystrokes", 2018), 50 changes hold 11.6 s of typing, about one short form of a name, an email address and a password (about 50 keystrokes, modelled).
  - The count, 50, is the smallest that holds such a form, a thin and modelled margin (about 49 keystrokes against 50), where 25 holds half of it (5.8 s); above it the edge is hard, because the producer sends `maxAge: 50` and the window trims to it, so 100 or 200 kept changes would be replayed into a window that keeps 50, and showing more needs the window's bound raised too, which multiplies its parsed copies (284.1 MB at 50 for the 5.53 MB cache, about 1.1 GB at 200); for the list, 100 and 200 cost 5.3 and 15.9 MB more than 50 for 12–35 s more typing. No count holds a whole drag, 60 changes a second at 60 Hz and 120 at 120 Hz, and 50 keep its last 0.83 s (0.42 s at 120 Hz), its end state included.
  - The limit, 16 MB per store at two bytes per character, leaves every store up to 160 KB its full 50, the 106 KB list using 5.3 million characters, 1.5 times below it, also with text outside Latin-1, which the page cannot detect without scanning every string; the 1.09 MB cache keeps 7 (7.7 MB, or 15.3 MB with two-byte text), and the 5.53 MB cache 1 while the window is open and none while it is closed. 8 MB would cut the list to 37 of its 50; 32 MB also keeps every ordinary store's 50 and doubles what large stores keep (14 changes of the 1.09 MB cache, 2 of the 5.53 MB cache), seven more changes of a large store, such as seven more 5 s polls (35 s), for up to twice the memory; the measurement fixes only the floor, 10.6 MB, at which the list keeps its 50 with two-byte text, and 16 MB is the smallest round value with margin above it.
  - Nearest alternatives: 25 halves the history to less than one short form to save at most 2.7 MB on ordinary stores, still holds 27–138 MB for large ones, and shows fewer changes from before the window opened than the window keeps after; 100 and 200 are cut back to 50 by the window unless its `maxAge` rises too, and hold 110 MB to 1.1 GB for large stores, 200 changes of the 5.53 MB cache taking a quarter of the page's measured 4.4 GB heap limit; a byte budget without the count lets the counter keep about 615,000 changes, about 92 MB of messages, of which the window shows 50; 50 without the limit holds 54.7–108.5 MB for a 1.1 MB state and 276.3 MB for a 5.5 MB one from registration, and the earlier estimate of about 50 MB for a 1 MB state holds only for Latin-1 text.
  - Pitfalls: serialization moves to every change from registration: a store set on every pointer move would spend 2.4%, 17% and 100% of the main thread on it for the list, the 1.09 MB cache and the 5.53 MB cache, so a 5.5 MB store changed every frame drops frames as soon as it registers, which the limit cannot prevent because a change must be serialized to be kept, hence the guard, which reads the length of the last payload the page already has; while it is open the window keeps its own parsed copy, 1.03–1.15 times the strings for states of 100 KB and more (57.2 MB for the 1.09 MB cache, 284.1 MB for the 5.53 MB one), and a popup its opener can script runs in the opener's renderer process, so the page's buffer and the window's history add up in one process, about 112 MB for the 1.09 MB cache at 50 each; a poll that returns the same data still notifies (12 of 12 polls), so without one string for equal payloads a polled store fills its limit with copies of one state; replaying K kept changes needs the state before the oldest as well, so the page holds at most the limit plus one state; keeping snapshot objects instead of strings costs 45–56 times less for the caches' updates, which share structure, and serializes nothing until a window connects, but a fresh poll response shares nothing and costs about its parsed size, a store mutated in place shows its current values in older entries, and a connecting window waits while the whole buffer is serialized at once (50 × 16.7 ms ≈ 0.8 s for the 5.53 MB cache), so it is an option for the implementation, not a reason to change the count; the limit is per store, so a page with several large stores holds up to 16 MB for each.
  - Consequence: three clicks before the window opens show as three `zustand/setState` changes after `@@INIT`; after 60 changes the window lists the latest 50 and says so; a store with a 1.1 MB state shows its latest 7 with "Zustand cache: showing its latest 7 changes", and one with a 5.5 MB state keeps none until the window connects, which the window says.
  - Backend work: none in Rust; third-party-devtools' Backend 6 (the page's buffer) ships the count, the limit, one string for equal consecutive payloads, the guard and the window's line together, because the buffer alone would hold 55–276 MB for large stores from registration and serialize every change of a store the page cannot afford; contract change to the Zustand-history sentence of `integrations/redux.md`. Until then a store's history starts when the window connects, as today.
  - Also open: 16 MB against 32 MB per store is a memory budget the product owner may adjust later; the measurement fixes only the floor at which ordinary stores keep their 50.
  - Applied by: third-party-devtools (Backend 6).
- **O50 Whether devhost starts the editor's command itself, and what Open does in another environment. Withdrawn (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q60a4 — no editor support): devhost never starts an editor (owner comment verbatim: “drop this feature”), and editor support is removed completely (`O51`, owner item q61a4: “drop editors completely”).** Supersedes the recommendation (starting the chosen editor's command locally via `POST /__devhost__/editor/open` and using a remote URL template or `"The file is on <machine>"` in another environment). From component-source's open conflict on `source-and-minimap-02`, reworked under `HOLD-8` (owner item q60). For (superseded recommendation's case): a page cannot see whether an editor's URL handler ran, so today Open copies and opens without a word and an editor that is not installed fails silently (`FB-5`, `FAIL-2`); starting the command would have let devhost report a missing command or a failed exit for files in the stack's worktrees, while a browser elsewhere would have used a remote editor URL. Against: `guides/react-highlight.md` says "devhost does not launch editors and ships no editor plugin."; starting processes adds a write route and separate command-line launchers; the owner chose to drop the feature and drop editor support completely (`O51`).
  - Earlier default (superseded): revision 3 had recommended the same command for a browser on this machine and said nothing about other machines; revision 4 added what a paired browser elsewhere got under `HOLD-8`.
  - Consequence: devhost never starts an editor, exposes no `/__devhost__/editor/open` route, and offers no **Open in Editor** action anywhere (`O51`); component source inspection keeps **Copy Path**.
  - Backend work: none; component-source's Backend 2 (`POST /__devhost__/editor/open`), its remote-editor fallback (`"The file is on devbox"`), and the proposed contract changes to `guides/react-highlight.md`, `guides/devtools.md` and `reference/control-api.md` beside `CC-37` and `CC-39` are withdrawn.
  - Applied by: component-source (withdraws Backend 2 and every **Open in Editor** hand-off; **Copy Path** stays); stack-directory and worktree-switch drop **Open in Editor** links.
- **O51 Editors beyond the four built-in ones. Withdrawn (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q61a4 — no editor support): editor support is removed completely (owner comment verbatim: “drop editors completely”) — no **Open in Editor**, no editor choice in the manifest or the devhost config file (`[devtools.editor]`, `DEVHOST_EDITOR`), and no URL or command templates; **Copy Path** stays. Retires `O60` (owner item q70).** Supersedes the recommendation (URL templates with `{file}`, `{line}`, `{column}`, `{host}` in the manifest, devhost config file and `DEVHOST_EDITOR`, plus command templates later). From component-source's open conflict on `source-and-minimap-03` (owner item q61). For (superseded recommendation's case): only four editors exist today (`cursor`, `vscode`, `vscode-insiders` and `webstorm`; `apps/devhost/src/manifest/validation.rs:85`) and the choice lives in the team's shared manifest; moving it to the person's own config with URL templates would have avoided manifest edits (`SEL-11`). Against: maintaining built-in editor URL schemes, command launchers, and templates across local and remote environments adds ongoing surface area; the owner chose to drop editors completely.
  - Consequence: component source inspection shows file, line, column and **Copy Path** only, with no editor menu, no **Open in Editor** action, and no editor settings in `devhost.toml`, `config.toml` or `DEVHOST_EDITOR`; `O60` (owner item q70) is retired.
  - Backend work: component-source's Backend 3 (editor templates and validation) and the proposed template contract changes beside `CC-39` are withdrawn; the editor parts of `CC-39` (`[devtools.editor]`, `DEVHOST_EDITOR`, `"and your own editor"`, and its editor-choice roster entry) are withdrawn while toolbar placement and shortcuts in `CC-39` stay; removing the existing editor UI, manifest key and docs (`guides/react-highlight.md`, `guides/devtools.md`) replaces them in Phase 8.
  - Applied by: component-source (withdraws step 6's editor settings and Backend 3; keeps **Copy Path**); `O60` retired; `O36`, `O59` and `O61` drop all editor references.
- **O52 Whether devhost writes a starter manifest. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q62a1): yes: `devhost init` writes a commented `devhost.toml` in the current folder with one service guessed from `package.json`'s `dev` script, `Cargo.toml`, `go.mod` or a `Procfile`, refuses to overwrite an existing file, and the message for a folder without a manifest offers it on its own line with the Quickstart's URL.** Approved as recommended. From stack-run-stop's open conflict on `stack-run-stop-12`, listed as also open in `O44` until Phase 7 put it to the product owner as owner item q62; the default is the one stack-run-stop's proposal assumes (Backend 16). For: nothing writes a manifest today. The commands are `start`, `stop`, `restart-all`, `stack`, `caddy`, `serve`, `skill`, `remote`, `doctor` and `config` (`apps/devhost/src/main.rs:42-93`, `apps/devhost/src/cli.rs`, `apps/devhost/src/serve_cli.rs`); the config editor edits manifests that exist, and `devhost serve` needs none. In a folder without one, `devhost start` searches that folder and each parent up to the first folder holding `.git` (`apps/devhost/src/manifest.rs:60-93`, `resolve_path`) and fails with "Could not find devhost.toml from <folder> upward." and "Run devhost from your project folder, or pass --manifest <path>.", which sends a person already in their project folder nowhere. The Quickstart in `devhost --help` runs "the stack in devhost.toml" without saying how to write one, and `guides/stack-lifecycle.md` has people copy a two-service example ("Describe your services in `devhost.toml`:"). first-run-setup's proposal leaves the Quickstart step that creates the manifest to stack-run-stop. Peers write a starter file and never overwrite one: `bun init` "Creates a package.json, tsconfig.json, and bunfig.toml if they don't exist.", `cargo init` in a folder that has a `Cargo.toml` stops with "`cargo init` cannot be run on existing Cargo packages", and `npm init` creates a `package.json` (local `--help` output and a `cargo init` run in a scratch folder, 2026-11-18); `stack-run-stop-12` also names `fly launch`. Against: one more command to document and keep working; the guess can be wrong (a stack of several services gets one, and a monorepo's root `package.json` may not start the dev server), so the file's comments must say what to check; `stack-run-stop-12`'s acceptance criteria are met by the docs URL alone, so a message and a Quickstart step that link the docs' example remove the dead end without a command.
  - Pitfall: the search stops at the repository's root, so stack-run-stop's proposed message "No devhost.toml in ~/newapp or any folder above it" overstates it inside a repository, and `guides/stack-lifecycle.md`'s "devhost uses the nearest `devhost.toml` in the current directory or a parent" omits that boundary today (a docs defect, not a code one: the stop is deliberate and tested). The message names where the search stopped (exact words per 2.12), and the docs correction goes with this entry's contract change.
  - Consequence: in `~/newapp`, a Bun project without a manifest, `devhost start` says that no `devhost.toml` was found, prints `devhost init` on its own line and the Quickstart's URL; `devhost init` writes `devhost.toml` with one commented service running `bun run dev`, names the file and what to check, and run again refuses and names the existing file (exact words per 2.12).
  - Backend work: the `devhost init` half of stack-run-stop's Backend 16 (its other half, `devhost skill` printing the embedded skill, needs a contract change but no product decision); a contract change to `reference/cli.md`, `guides/stack-lifecycle.md` and the Quickstart in `devhost --help` (a `CC-n` to add, as stack-run-stop's [discuss] contract item asks); the Rust CLI test of stack-run-stop's criterion.
  - Applied by: stack-run-stop (step 2's message for a folder without a manifest, step 16's Quickstart, Backend 16 and its criterion); first-run-setup's Quickstart, which keeps Caddy's steps first and leaves the step that creates the manifest to stack-run-stop.
- **O53 Whether a success notice's time also stops while the browser window is not focused. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q63a3, reversing the recommendation): yes, only for a notice that arrives while the browser window is not focused — its countdown starts when the person next focuses the browser window, while a notice that arrived while the window was focused keeps counting as `O14` says (pausing only when the pointer or focus is in the notice or the page is hidden). The notice stack reads `document.hasFocus()` on `blur`, `focus` and `visibilitychange`, never `blur` alone so embedded frames inside the app do not pause it.** Supersedes the recommendation (no: stop only while the page is hidden). It refines `O14`'s calibrated decision (the longer of 4 s and 1.5 s plus 0.3 s per word, at most 15 s; the Undo 4 s; owner item q24a3). For (superseded recommendation's case): a visible page is read without clicking it, which is how people watch a page while editing (save, glance, save again), so a notice in view counts down; a background tab, a minimized window and a cover macOS detects already stop the time (the cases below); every outcome of a save or restart stays in the stack's log (`FB-7`), and the outcomes without a log line are page actions taken in the browser (copying, sharing the tab) and reconnects (`O14`'s calibration); stopping on focus for every notice keeps successes beside the bar until the person clicks into the page, so repeated saves pile up in `FB-8`'s fourth group; sonner 2.0.8 stops only while `document.hidden`. Against (why the owner chose q63a3): outcomes of a save made in the editor ("… after devhost.toml changed on disk") arrive while the browser is not focused and can close before the person looks, and a browser fully covered by a same-size window of another application can count as visible (below); holding only notices that arrive while unfocused catches editor-driven saves without holding notices the person already saw before switching away. What the page sees in each case a person meets, measured in HeadlessChrome 157 through agent-browser 0.43.0 on a local `file://` probe page (2026-11-18), and cited where headless Chromium cannot produce the case:
  - Another tab in front (measured): the window's `blur`, then `visibilitychange` to `hidden`; `document.hasFocus()` false. Brought back: `visible`, then `focus`.
  - Window minimized (measured, through CDP `Browser.setWindowBounds`): `blur` and `hidden`; restored: `focus` and `visible`.
  - Fully covered, or on another desktop (cited, macOS; not measured): Chromium marks the page occluded about 1 s after macOS reports that its window has no visible part (`content/app_shim_remote_cocoa/web_contents_view_cocoa.mm`, `kOcclusionUpdateDelayInSeconds = 1.0`), and `WebContentsImpl::CalculatePageVisibilityState` reports every state but visible as `hidden` unless a capture keeps the tab visible (Chromium `main`, read 2026-11-18). Apple defines the window's occlusion state as "If set, at least part of the window is visible; if not set, the entire window is occluded", so a window on another desktop is expected hidden. Chromium's own extra check (`components/remote_cocoa/app_shim/window_occlusion_checker_mac.h`) exists for "windows of similar size, a case macOS's occlusion detection system cannot handle", walks only `-[NSApplication orderedWindows]` (Chromium's own windows) and is unsupported on macOS 13.0 to 13.2, so a browser fully covered by another application's window of about the same size, such as a maximized editor, can stay `visible`.
  - Beside or partly behind the editor (cited; a second headless window in front left the first page `visible` with `document.hasFocus()` true, so headless cannot produce it): `visible` and not focused, because the HTML standard's has-focus steps return false when "target's node navigable's top-level traversable does not have system focus". This is the case `O53` changes.
  - Focus moved into a frame inside the app (measured): the top window receives `blur` while its `document.hasFocus()` stays true, as the has-focus steps' walk into child navigables says.
  - Pitfall: a pause keyed to the window's `blur` event, as Radix's is, also pauses whenever focus moves into a frame inside the app, such as an embedded payment form or preview (measured above); therefore the notice stack reads `document.hasFocus()` at arrival and on `blur`, `focus` and `visibilitychange`, never `blur` alone.
  - Consequence: with the browser beside the editor, "Applied devhost.toml: restarted api (changed) and worker (depends on api) · 6 of 6 running · 3.1 s" arrives while the browser window is unfocused and waits until the person next focuses the browser, then counts down its 6.9 s; a notice that arrived while the browser was focused keeps counting if the person switches away while the page remains visible; in a background tab or a minimized window its time still waits until the page shows again.
  - Backend work: none; the notice stack reads both page visibility and `document.hasFocus()` at arrival and on `blur`, `focus` and `visibilitychange` (`FB-6`).
  - Applied by: notices-and-feedback (the notice stack's lifetime, `FB-6`) and the eight other flows whose successes close by themselves (`O14`'s calibration: services-panel, manifest-hot-reload, config-editor, startup-and-recovery, worktree-switch, reload-control, component-source and page-context).
- **O54 Whether an authenticated remote browser in another environment may save the configuration. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q64a1): yes: with the config editor on, saves are accepted from this machine and from authenticated remote browsers (passkey or paired, `O36`); the editor stays off by default; the relay connector's blanket refusal of config writes becomes the same session check; unauthenticated clients can neither read (`O38`) nor save.** Approved as recommended. From the product owner's earlier rule that the config editor saves only from a browser on the same machine (section 1's owner-settled intents), which Phase 7 put back to the product owner as owner item q64 because `HOLD-8` forbids confining a feature to this machine; "off by default" is untouched. For: the earlier rule's reason is that saving a manifest decides which commands devhost runs (`guides/devtools.md`: "Only a browser on the same machine can save, even when Caddy listens on all interfaces."; the `403 Forbidden` row of `reference/control-api.md`'s status table, "or from another machine"; `apps/devhost/src/devtools/config_files.rs:265-295`, reaffirmed by `main`'s relay, `apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs:21-71`); once remote use needs an authenticated browser, an authenticated person can already change the same files through chat, because an agent edits a manifest or the devhost config file in place and hot reload applies it, so keeping saves on this machine stops nothing an authenticated person cannot do another way and keeps out only unauthenticated clients, which `O36` already keeps out. Against: it overturns an explicit owner rule restated in code while remote use was built; a save from another environment changes what runs on the stack's machine at once, behind the Configuration window's own review before Save rather than an agent's approval prompt; the alternative kept saves on this machine as an exception to `HOLD-8`, with **Save** unavailable elsewhere and its reason (`HOLD-7`).
  - Consequence: a person in another environment with an authenticated remote browser edits and saves in the Configuration window, and the reload's outcome names what restarted; on this machine nothing changes; a chat turn that edits a manifest ends with hot reload applying it either way.
  - Backend work: the save's `is_local_client` (`apps/devhost/src/devtools/config_files.rs:269`) becomes `O36`'s check (this machine or authenticated remote browser), which `canSave` reports; the relay connector's refusal becomes the session check; contract changes to `guides/devtools.md` (the sentence above), the status table of `reference/control-api.md` and `stacks/remote-previews.md` (its sentence that the connector rejects changes to the configuration editor through remote routes). Ships with `O36`.
  - Applied by: config-editor; `HOLD-7`, `WORD-7`'s refusal example and `review-16` follow this decision; proxy-settings (its "saving only from this machine" line).
- **O55 Where Watch Live Activity opens so that a browser in another environment can use it. Decided (product owner, 2026-11-18, owner item q65a1): also on the stack directory's hostname (`[caddy].stackDirectoryHost`, `devhost.localhost` by default), never an app's, answered for this machine and authenticated remote browsers (passkey or paired, `O36`); `http://127.0.0.1:<port>` keeps working on this machine; the relay forwards the stack directory's hostname.** Approved as recommended. From the product owner's earlier rule that Watch Live Activity lives on `http://127.0.0.1:<port>` so page scripts cannot read transcripts (section 1's owner-settled intents), which Phase 7 put back to the product owner as owner item q65: `HOLD-8` leaves "never on the app's hostname" standing, but the loopback address confines the feature, because from another environment `127.0.0.1` is that environment's own machine. For: transcripts can hold anything an agent read, secrets included (`guides/devtools.md`: "transcripts can contain anything an agent read, including secrets"; `reference/control-api.md`: "Live agent activity answers only at `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, because application scripts can read anything on a routed hostname."; `apps/devhost/src/devtools/agent_activity.rs:25-54`); what keeps app scripts from them is a separate origin, not the address, and the stack directory's hostname is an origin no app shares, so app scripts cannot read it while `O36` lets it answer an authenticated remote browser. Against: the relay must forward one more hostname (`apps/devhost/src/remote/proxy.rs:21-71` forwards active stacks' hostnames only), the same change `O47` and `O36`'s reserved-host sign-in hop need; transcripts reach every browser the person signs in on; the alternative kept the page on `127.0.0.1` only, as an exception to `HOLD-8`, unavailable in another environment with its reason, where the person follows only chat's own conversations.
  - Consequence: in an authenticated remote browser in another environment, **Watch Live Activity** opens on the stack directory's hostname and follows the worktree's agents, those started in a terminal included; on this machine it still opens on `127.0.0.1` as well; no app page ever hosts it; `FB-12` does not depend on it, since chat's own turns show in the chat panel (`O36`).
